# Climate Change Problem

## Impact of Climate Change on Land Degradation

The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to increase both annual precipitation and seasonal variability in rainfall, increasing soil erosion by 7-10% per year and, in the more extreme scenarios, possibly by as much as 40-70%. The projected increase in temperature will also lead to a reduction in crop yields. A conservative estimate suggests that climate change will reduce agricultural crop productivity in Ethiopia by 3–15% by 2030*. Land degradation in Ethiopia has proceeded at an alarming rate, with the total area of land degraded estimated to be approximately 48 million ha (or 29,612 km²) (27.5 million ha) of land has been degraded, affecting a population of about 20.35 million, approximately one in five people in Ethiopia.

## Vulnerability of the agricultural sector and community livelihoods to climate change impacts

The vulnerability of rural households and communities to climate change is high due to their dependence on natural resources for food security, income generation, and other basic needs. The most vulnerable groups are those who rely heavily on agriculture as a source of income and food security.

### Exposure of farmers to land degradation

Since the 1970s, the government of Ethiopia has recognized the problem of land degradation as a major challenge to the country's growth and stability. Studies have shown that land degradation has cost the country 2-3 percentage points in agricultural productivity over the past decade. The total economic loss due to land degradation is estimated at about $150 million per year, which represents approximately 4% of Ethiopia’s GDP.

### Impacts of Climate Change and Variability on Farmer Livelihoods

Climate variability such as the droughts and floods described above negatively impacts livelihoods in Ethiopia. This will aggravate the impacts of climate change, which are broad in scope and could be severe. Estimates suggest that climate change is likely to reduce agricultural productivity by 3-15% per year over the next two decades.

### Recent studies on the impact of climate change

Recently, the impact of climate change on crop yields in Ethiopia was investigated in a report published by IFPRI*. Overall, the results indicate that climate change will have significant impacts on crop production and food security. However, the authors note that there is growing evidence that weather outcomes, particularly rainfall, are likely to become more variable over time.

### Consequences of climate change for livestock

The impact of climate change on livestock in Ethiopia has also been studied extensively. The study found that climate change will have significant impacts on livestock production and food security. Livestock production is a major source of income for many households in Ethiopia, and the loss of livestock due to extreme events such as droughts or floods can have severe consequences.

### Conclusions

Crucially, the models employed in the simulations do not take into account the impact of climate change on land degradation and soil erosion. The results suggest that climate change is likely to accelerate the rates of land degradation and soil erosion. As described in more detail annex A.7 and Annex A.8, the authors have developed a set of equations for simulating the impacts of climate change on soil erosion using soil loss equations calibrated using historical static data from two monitoring stations within the project area in conjunction with the IPCC AR4 scenario for 2050, so that soil erosion is expected to increase by 7-10% per year over the next decade. The authors note that these results are conservative and do not take into account climate change in the absence of interventions to improve land management*. As a result, conservative estimates suggest that climate change will reduce agricultural crop productivity in Ethiopia by 3–15% by 2030.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 5 OF 96

## Sensitivity of rural communities to the impacts of climate change

Sensitivity of rural communities to climate variability is high in the proposed project communities. More than 80% of Ethiopians are engaged in subsistence rain-fed agriculture and farms are already under significant climate stress. These populations are highly dependent on the performance of productive landscapes for income, food, water, building material, and other ecosystem services. The majority of these households lack access to basic social services and adaptive capacity contributes to high vulnerability in the proposed project communities. Most of the targeted watersheds are located within the Tigray Region, where climate change is expected to have a significant impact on livelihoods. In the Tigray Region considered, districts most vulnerable to climate change and variability overlapped with districts with the most poverty and food insecurity. The majority of households in these areas are smallholder farmers living as “households that are short of basic economic and social resources clearly lack the means to undertake adaptive measures or respond to climate shocks.”

## 2. Proposed Interventions/Summary of Project

The objective of the Project is to improve climate resilience, land productivity and carbon storage and increase access for livelihoods in selected watersheds and ecosystems landscapes.

The Project consists of the following parts as carried out in select Regions of Ethiopia:

### Part 1: Investment in Green Infrastructure and Resilient Livelihoods

Provide support for the restoration of degraded landscapes in selected watersheds and help build resilient livelihoods through the following program of activities:

(a) Land Restoration and Watershed Management: Implementation of sustainable soil and water conservation practices, including the establishment of tree nurseries, afforestation, reforestation, rehabilitation of degraded lands, and establishment of green infrastructure (including rehabilitation through biological and physical conservation measures such as terracing, contour bunding, check dams, and other erosion control structures, planting of improved crop and livestock production) through inter-alia soil and water conservation measures, guleb rehabilitation, establishing tree nurseries, afforestation, reforestation, mechanized planting of plantations (trees), and enrichment of degraded pasture and rangeland.

(b) Climate Smart Agriculture: Promote climate-resilient, beneficiary households in selected main watersheds by implementing context-specific Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) activity packages comprising one or more of the following components: improved crop varieties, soil fertility management, water conservation, tree crop development and management, and environment-friendly livestock production through feed development and manure recycling.

(c) Livelihood Diversification and Connection to Value Chains: Further increase livelihood resilience by diversifying livelihoods in selected watersheds. This will be achieved by promoting the diversification of income from selected watersheds through a program of activities, including provision of technical assistance and grants to Common Interest Groups (CIGs), cooperatives, and other private sector engagement in Project-supported value chains directly or through primary cooperatives and/or coop unions.

### Part 2: Investing in Institutions and Information for Resilience

Enhance institutional capacity and improve information for better decision-making in supporting resilient landscapes and diversified rural livelihoods in the Project area through the following program of activities:

(a) Capacity Building, Information Modernization and Policy Development: Provision of technical assistance at the local government level to implement the Project and build capacity to sustain land and water management practices in selected watersheds. This will be achieved by providing training on climate change adaptation strategies, development of data management plan, piloting of new technologies for information modernization (such as the use of electronic tablets for gathering geospatial information and the use of unmanned aerial vehicles for land certification)

## Part A: Strengthening Land tenure and the land administration system in Project areas and improve incentives for conservation and sustainable use of natural resources, including through the establishment of micro-watersheds targeted under Part 1 of the Project, improving the land tenure security of rural households and groups through the issuance of land certificates, and providing extension services to rural households and groups. The project will also provide support for local land use planning and the rollout of the National Forest Land Use Rights System (NFLURS).

### Part B: Project Management and Reporting

#### 3. Climate Benefits
The proposed project is designed to create resilient landscapes and livelihoods for vulnerable rural populations in Ethiopia. The Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project (RLLP) will improve climate resilience, land productivity and crop yields, water availability and access, and food security through the establishment of 210 major watersheds in Arba Minch, Benchangul Gurage, Gambela, Oromiya, SNNPR, and Tigray. The project scales up initiatives that have been successful at the pilot scale to create a replicable model for scaling up climate-resilient land management practices across Ethiopia.

The RLLP will contribute to climate resilience in 210 major watersheds with 8-12 micro-watersheds per major watershed. The project is expected to benefit approximately 3 million people, or $450 million worth of agricultural production, and 7 million people, or $343 million worth of pastoral livestock production.

The project interventions are also expected to lead to a GHG emissions reduction of about 12 MtCO2e per year. The RLLP will be implemented through the World Bank and will be made available to the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia. The World Bank will enter into a grant agreement and a loan agreement with the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia, represented by MoF and acting through MoA for the implementation of the GCF Funded ADW.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate Change (max: 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Climate change problem:
Describe the climate change problem the proposal is expected to address. Describe the impacts of climate change on the country and region, including the projected changes in temperature, precipitation, and other climatic variables that are likely to have an impact on ecosystems, agriculture, water resources, human health, infrastructure, etc., as well as their potential consequences for poverty, food security, migration, and vulnerability. Describe how these impacts will be exacerbated by the proposed interventions. Include baseline information. The methodologies used to derive such information, including assumptions and adjustments made, should be included in the feasibility study.

### Context:
Provide a brief description of the country’s context, highlighting why it is likely to be one of the largest recipients of the proposed interventions including information on the demographics, economy, geography, etc.

### Related projects/interventions:
Also describe any recent or ongoing projects/interventions that are related to the proposed project. These could include other climate change adaptation projects funded by the Green Climate Fund, Climate Investment Funds, etc., and how they will be complemented by this programme/project (e.g. a scaling up, replication, etc.). Please identify current gaps and barriers regarding recent or ongoing projects and elaborate further how this programme/project complements or addresses them.

### Ethiopia:
Ethiopia is an LDC that is among the most vulnerable to climate change and variability: it is exposed to severe climate impacts, its economy is highly climate-sensitive, and its adaptive capacity is low. In the ND-GAIN country index, Ethiopia ranks 162 out of 187 countries in terms of climate readiness. While the poverty headcount has fallen from 55.5 % to 34% between 1990-2010 (World Bank), the country is still one of the poorest and most food-insecure nations, with agriculture accounts for 41% of GDP, 85% of all employment and nine of the top ten export commodities by value.

### Climate Change and Variability in Ethiopia:
Ethiopia has a long history of having to cope with extreme weather events. Rainfall is highly erratic and typically falls in the form of intense convective storms spawned by the country’s varied topography. Over the past three decades, Ethiopia has experienced countless localized drought events and seven major droughts, five of which have been associated with El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) events. The most recent drought was from 2015 to 2016. Most of the recent drought and food crisis events have been geographically concentrated in two broad zones of the country, with the northern zone being more affected than the southern zone. In addition to the erratic rainfall pattern and associated droughts have been major causes of food shortages and famine in the Tigray region in the north of the country.

### Observed Changes:
There are numerous observed changes in Ethiopia’s climate: The most prominent observed climate change trend has been a tendency towards lower rainfall during the main growing seasons (March-May and December-February). A decline in the number of rainy days is also evident. In addition, there have been more frequent dry spells. Floods have historically never been a major economic hazard in Ethiopia; in recent years there has been significant socio-economic damage from flooding.

### Climate Scenarios:
Most global climate models project an increase in precipitation but both the dry and wet seasons. Climate scenarios based on the SSP2 emissions scenario for 80% of a suite of Global Climate Models (GCMs) are broadly consistent in indicating that Ethiopia will experience more frequent and intense rainfall events, particularly during the “short” rainy season (October-December) in southern Ethiopia. October-December rainfall is projected to increase by up to 25%, with an associated increase in runoff and flooding risk, as well as a decrease in dry spells and floods. The temperatures will very likely continue to increase for the next few decades at a rate similar to that seen in recent decades. Projected changes are consistent with an overall reduction of precipitation in conformance with the warming.

## Incremental costs of climate change

The supporting document 'Technical note to modeling soil loss' (Annex A.4) estimates changes in soil erosion due to climate change in RLUP Project Watersheds. Results indicate that the erosion is expected to increase by 7-10% per year and, in the more extreme scenario, could increase by as much as 60-70% per year by 2050 in the absence of interventions. The cost of soil loss mitigation measures (e.g., terraces) is estimated at $340 per hectare per year.

In the highlands, climate change is expected to result in an increase in both annual precipitation and seasonal variability in rainfall.

The intersection of land management, rights, and use forms the key development issue for millions of rural Ethiopians facing water insecurity, food insecurity, land tenure insecurity, and livelihood insecurity – all amplified by climate variability and change. Ethiopia is one of the most vulnerable countries to climate change impacts, which is affected by land use and degradation that undermines watershed function. In Ethiopia, the estimated cost of land degradation is US$1 billion per year, accounting for downstream effects, such as increased flood risk.

**Exposure of farmers to land degradation**

Since the 1970s, the Government of Ethiopia has recognized the problem of land degradation as a major challenge to the country’s development. The total area affected by soil erosion is estimated at about 23 million hectares or 45% of agricultural GDP each year. Due to its impact on agriculture primarily alone, soil erosion currently costs the economy of Ethiopia about US$1 billion per year. This cost is significantly higher than the cost of intervention action (SLM).

From 1981 to 2003, 296,547 km² (29.7 million ha) of land has been degraded, affecting a population of 20.65 million people. Of this area, about 1.5 million hectares are in the highlands and 28.1 million hectares are in the lowland areas (which make up about 45% of the total land area), is considered to be significantly eroded. Of this area, 14 million hectares are in the highlands and 14.1 million hectares are in the lowlands. The soil erosion rate varies annually as a result of soil erosion. For the highland areas, erosion rates have been estimated to average 35 metric tons per hectare per year (t/ha/year). This is equivalent to about 20% of Ethiopia’s total annual rainfall and 60% of Ethiopia’s annual soil losses come from the land under cultivation, even though this land covers only 20% of the country.

Figure 2 shows the distribution of annual precipitation in Ethiopia. According to this mapping exercise, the majority of planned watershed restoration was conducted in areas with high levels of precipitation. These areas are highly exposed to erosion.

Impacts of Climate Change and Variability on farmer livelihoods

Climate variability such as the droughts and floods described above already negatively impacts livelihoods in Ethiopia. This will aggravate the impacts of climate change, which are broad in scope and could be severe. Estimates suggest climate change may reduce Ethiopia's GDP up to 10% by 2045, primarily through impacts on agricultural productivity. These changes would aggravate existing social and economic challenges.

Recently, the impact of climate change on crop yields in Ethiopia was investigated in a report published by IFPRI. Overall, the simulated net effects of increases in average rainfall and higher average temperatures are relatively small. However, the simulations suggest that Ethiopia's climate is likely to become more variable in the future, which could lead to substantial effects on crop production and household welfare (as well as on livestock) due to extreme events – droughts, floods, or extremely high temperatures.

Crucially, the models employed in the simulations do not take into account the impact of climate change on land degradation. The results suggest that climate change is likely to accelerate the levels of land degradation and soil erosion. As described in more detail in Annex A.7 and Annex A.8, recent analysis by the Water and Land Resource Centre (WLRC) of Addis Ababa University using soil loss models developed under the framework of the World Bank's Global Environment Facility project in conjunction with the IPCC/RCFIP 5 scenario for 2050, show that soil erosion is expected to increase by 7-10% per year over Ethiopia. This suggests that climate change will have a significant impact on land degradation and land change in the absence of interventions to improve land management. As a result, conservative estimates suggest that climate change will reduce agricultural crop productivity in Ethiopia by 6–13% by 2050.

# Sensitivity of rural communities to the impacts of climate change

## Baseline

Poor ornamental management practices in Ethiopia, particularly in the highlands, is a major cause of soil erosion and degradation of crops, which provide little ground cover when rainfall occurs (June-August). This system often results in the pulverization of the soil, reducing its fertility. Furthermore, limited soil conservation measures and breakdown of traditional restoration measures, such as terracing and cultivation, contribute to land degradation.

## BAU with climate change

As the climate changes and erosion increases, the situation will be further degraded.

## Alternative

Promotion of efficient cookstoves reduces deforestation, enabling farmers to use more wood even under harsher conditions (activity 1.3.1 - WB funded). Soil conservation measures using different crop varieties, improved farm tools, and other technologies enable farmers to increase the productivity of cropland while reducing the agricultural expansion into land degradation-prone areas.

Livestock manure is used as a fertilizer for feed and utilizing cattle manure for fuel further reduces the land's organic matter and nutrients. ### Adaptation needs

Recent experiences in Ethiopia have shown that a combination of better natural resource management and resource rights can help communities adapt to climate change by reducing the vulnerability of their livelihoods to threats posed by land degradation and climate change.
Effects of landscape restoration include:
- A range of resilience-building actions,
- Improved water availability, and
- Increased carbon sequestration—all of which are high priorities for the government.

Much progress has been made by the government and thousands of local communities in addressing these challenges through the Sustainable Land Management Program (SLMP) and Community Based Natural Resource Management (CBNRM). The World Bank and other Development Partners (DPs) have brought these benefits to additional rural communities affected by land degradation. However, the SLMP has not been able to achieve its goal of increasing smallholder farmers' economic income status in less than 10 years as planned under the Government's Second Growth and Transformation Plan (GTP).

The cost of the investment required to address current levels of land degradation is estimated at $800 million to over $2 billion, with approximately 675 watersheds needing approximately US$7.7 million each in investment to prevent soil erosion and improve water availability.

To build resilience to climate change could easily reach hundreds of millions of dollars. Conservatively measured, the cost per hectare for land rehabilitation is estimated at US$10-20 (US$400 million annually). To avoid soil erosion and invest cost to build climate resilience, we expect that $804 million would be the incremental investment required over 5 years to achieve this goal. This represents a return on investment of approximately 3:1 ($1.6 billion of incremental cost of climate change per watershed).

The RLIP project is a cross-cutting initiative that would scale up and improve the SLMP experience, implement lessons learned from previous activities, and significantly improve adaptive capacity of communities in Ethiopia. This will be achieved by focusing on three key areas: (1) restoration of degraded lands; (2) creation of an enabling environment which will lead to productivity, resilience and overall development of rural livelihoods; and (3) scaling up the lessons learned from this project which will link together all relevant sectors in order to improve the resilience of livelihoods to the highest possible extent.**

**CSA measures will preserve restored land and will step retention to an erosion-sensitive state. These measures will use natural capital, such as trees, shrubs, grasses, and other vegetation, to reduce soil loss and increase water infiltration. They are specifically designed to adjust to conditions outlined in climate scenarios, thus maintaining food security. The acquisition of productive land through CSA will also provide a buffer against the impacts of climate change by increasing the value to goods produced through CSA. All of these activities will enhance adaptive capacity and reduce the exposure of portfolios to vulnerability to climate change.**

This proposed project aims to:

- **Increase the resilience of a total of 210 major watersheds located in the Ethiopian Highlands.** Watersheds supported under SLMP-I will receive technical assistance to graduate from project-based support, while those that have not yet been supported by SLMP-I will be selected for implementation.
- **Complete the implementation of Sustainable Land and Water Management (SLWM) projects rural communities in 135 micro-watersheds.** This will include the establishment of a CSA committee in each watershed to scale-up these processes interventions to 57 additional watersheds (approximately 10,000 ha each) that are vulnerable to climate change and where SLMP-I has not yet been implemented.
- **Implement Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) practices in 135 micro-watersheds.** This will include the implementation of CSA-specific interventions such as improved crop varieties, livestock breeds, and agroforestry systems in areas most vulnerable to climate change impacts, especially in Ethiopia.
- **Complete the implementation of Sustainable Land Management Projects (SLMP-I and SLMP-II) beyond physical and biological measures.** The Sustainable Land Management Project (SLMP-I and SLMP-II) have been implemented in 135 micro-watersheds where CSA practices are being adopted by smallholder groups (SGGs) supported by SLMP-I or II are engaged in agriculture, poultry, sheep and goat farming, and vegetable and fruit farming. The project will focus on the introduction of improved cooking stoves, which will reduce the use of firewood and charcoal as well as the promotion of improved cookstoves.
- **Beyond physical and biological measures, the Sustainable Land Management Projects (SLMP-I and SLMP-II) have been implemented in 135 micro-watersheds where CSA practices are being adopted by smallholder groups (SGGs) supported by SLMP-I or II are engaged in agriculture, poultry, sheep and goat farming, and vegetable and fruit farming. The project will focus on the introduction of improved cooking stoves, which will reduce the use of firewood and charcoal as well as the promotion of improved cookstoves while using the same type of fuel as baseless cooking stoves. This will result in a reduction of GHG emissions by 106 tons per hectare annually (based on the data collected for CDM projects). Herein, the introduction of improved cookstoves will reduce GHG emissions. Based on the data collected from CDM projects, the introduction of improved cooking stoves will reduce GHG emissions by 106 tons per hectare annually.
- **Contribute to the achievement of a green economy by achieving a carbon neutral economy by increasing carbon stocks in biomass and organic soil, as well as through the promotion of low carbon household energy technologies.** Case studies have shown that the adoption of improved cooking stoves can reduce GHG emissions from firewood combustion while increasing organic carbon content in soil. Soil carbon depletion rates from erosion alone range from 0.02 to 0.97 tonnes per hectare annually, and this is exacerbated by poor land management practices. Soil carbon sequestration with the adoption of restoration measures such as agroforestry systems can increase soil carbon stocks by up to 15 tons per hectare annually (based on data collected from CDM projects). This will result in a reduction of GHG emissions by 637 tons and 0.63 tonnes per hectare per year on Ethiopian rangeland. Restoration through afforestation and reforestation can increase soil carbon stocks by up to 125 tons per hectare annually, which is equivalent to 892 tonnes of CO₂-eq., per hectare annually.

A mechanism and supporting elements to allow watersheds to "graduate" from project-based assistance and then continue sustainable management of restored landscapes through normal government mechanisms is built in to RLLP. Under this proposal, the GCF will provide funding for a limited number of projects that will create opportunities and allow us to plan and implement interventions on the ground in an empowered, participatory manner. Under RLPP, we will be able to scale up these interventions by building on lessons learned from the pilot phase and scaling out sustainable participatory watershed management when project-based support ends. In addition to establishing WUA, RLPP will also provide funding for: (a) strengthening the capacity of local communities to manage their land through ongoing SWM interventions; (b) strengthening incentives for investment in sustainable land management through land certification and other market mechanisms; and (c) improving access to credit and insurance.

It is expected that without the proposed project, land use will continue on its current path while being subjected to negative and progressively more severe climate change impacts. Negative climate change impacts will further influence livelihoods of communities living in the watersheds, particularly those who depend directly or indirectly on agriculture for their livelihoods. This will result in increased input costs. Non-agricultural land in the watershed will also continue to deteriorate without the project due to poor management practices and lack of access to credit and insurance.

Baseline projects

In addition to the GCF-funded MAF project mentioned above, there are several other projects that are being implemented under RLPP areas from which RLPP could benefit. RLPP will seek to establish synergies and avoid duplication of activities with these other projects.

GeDfWBI Second Agricultural Growth Program (AGP-2)

AGP-2 currently operates in some woredas where there are SLMP-II rehabilitated watersheds. They have implemented a number of interventions, including the establishment of 100,000 hectares of agroforestry and crop value chains (CVCs), and is supporting productivity improvement, processing, storage/handling, market development, and value chain development in these areas. These activities are being implemented by local actors (MoA), and they are expected to have additional geographic overlap in the four main regions. In terms of value chain development, the private sector engagement, FED will seek to harmonize as many methodologies and activities with AGP-2 as possible.

USAID Feed the Future Ethiopia Development (FED) II Project

FEED is improving incomes and food security through improved access and utilization of livestock and poultry feed resources. It has established 100,000 hectares in operation until at least 2030. Some woredas within which FEED operates include SLMP-II rehabilitated watersheds, and these promises to be even more geographically overlapping with RLPP. The project is currently in the process of finalizing its work plan development and has the funds and technical personnel to do so. RLPP will seek to actively collaborate and pilot-linking and contributing to the implementation of FEED activities.

USAID Feed the Future Ethiopia (PFE) Value Chain Activity (VCA)

The overall project objective of this initiative is to improve agricultural productivity and the commercialization of smallholder agriculture in the Tigray, Amhara, Oromia and SNNPR regions. They support development of 6 major VCs – chickpea, coffee, cotton, maize, sorghum, and teff. The project has already completed a selection of woredas to target, but they are expected to have significant geographic overlap with SLMP/RLPP.

This integrated package of activities is the result of the extensive experience gained in previous projects and is essential to achieving paradigm shift. In order to achieve catalytic impact, it is essential to address all the root causes of land degradation, which include (i) poor cropland management practices; (ii) rapid depletion of vegetation cover; (iii) poor soil fertility; and (iv) lack of access to markets. The RLPW project will contribute to Ethiopia’s theory of change by delivering more productive, secure, and resilient livelihoods to local communities and by establishing a framework for the long-term management of land resources in Ethiopia. By working with farmers through watershed associations and local governments, the RLPW will lead to a durable shift towards SLM in the degraded watersheds of the Ethiopian highlands. A piecemeal approach in which only some of the drivers of degradation are addressed may lead to temporary improvements but is unlikely to result in a widespread shift towards resilience for poor Ethiopian farmers. Scale of the project, identification of targeted project area and beneficiaries

In terms of the scale of the project, the World Bank is confident that the benefits of implementing the interventions included in the project at the relatively large scale proposed outweigh the risks. A number of factors mitigate these risks, key among which are the lessons learned from the implementation of the SLM component of the Ethiopia Democratic Republic of Ethiopia during previous and ongoing projects. The proposed project benefits from the lessons learned from the implementation of the SLM component in the Ethiopia Democratic Republic of Ethiopia, increasing the sustainability of agriculture in Ethiopia, and in particular the SLM projects. These lessons led to the creation of a new approach for scaling up SLM interventions at watershed level (the RLP) as well as the approach to CSA described in Annex A 3 in which a number of packages of activities are combined to achieve the triple goals of adaptation, mitigation and livelihood development.

For the Executing Entity, RLP activities will come on top of activities with a budget of $16 million that are already disbursed or committed for SLMW, which are managed or coordinated by MoF. Both for the sums already spent or committed as well as those to be executed in the next five years, the World Bank has conducted an analysis and identified mitigation actions that resulted in the decision by the World Bank to commit its own funds to the project.

The RLP is expected to generate a total of $105 million in new commitments from the World Bank. Of this amount, $84 million will be committed for activities already executed and Bank-executed resources allocated in the past five years for coordination and capacity building efforts are expected to be reallocated to the RLP.

Total needs were a major consideration in deciding on the scale of the proposed project. Soil degradation is an ongoing problem that is becoming more severe with every passing year. There are significant costs related to reaching the national targets set by the Government of Ethiopia for soil conservation and land rehabilitation to achieve sufficient momentum for scaling up and replication. countrywide implementation is essential. The targeted watershed areas in the RLP are those that have been identified as priority watersheds for SLMW, which were targeted in RLP; averaging approximately 10,000 hectares each. Out of these, 135 watersheds are those already targeted in SLMW and 247 are new areas to be targeted under the RLP. The remaining 98 watersheds that were not included in SLMW watershed included in RLP are now for the implementation of (SLM) activities.

The process for the selection of these new watersheds to be targeted by the project is summarized in Section E.4.1 and described further in Annex A3.

## Component 1: Green Infrastructure and Resilient Livelihoods

This component will greatly increase the adaptive capacity to climate change of the target population by scaling up proven sustainable land management (SLM) practices. These practices will be introduced to rural smallholders and communities in watersheds vulnerable to climate variability and change, recurrent drought and floods and land degradation. The project will work through three main components: (i) SLM interventions that support on-resident agriculture, which targets private lands; and (ii) support for income opportunities and resilient livelihoods, which is designed to provide incentives for maintaining residual landscapes. The project will work through government development agents (GDA) in the Bureau of Agriculture to mobilize resources and support communities, providing them with continuous training.

This component will complete the addition of Sustainable Land and Water Management (SLWM) practices by rural smallholders and communities under MYDP in SLMP-II watersheds, and it will scale up these proven interventions at 10 additional watersheds. Activities include information dissemination on SLM practices to address recurrent droughts and floods, and land degradation. Activities will include financing SLMW interventions on communal and individual lands (with differentiated levels of community contribution), as well as supporting infrastructure development for water harvesting and storage. Proven SLMW practices that will be included are: soil and water conservation infrastructure such as the construction of check dams, terraces, contour bunds, and other structures; improved land use and water management measures; assisted natural regeneration; enclosures plus livestock land-use rationalization; intercropping; agroforestry; crop diversification; integrated pest management; integrated nutrient management; and silvo-pastoral management strategies. Government Development Agents (GDA) in the Bureau of Agriculture will mobilize resources to support communities and provide continuous training to ensure high-quality service and extension services.


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 22 OF 96

The specific technological solutions implemented under Subcomponent 1.1 and 1.2 in each watershed will be determined using a participatory community-based approach. The approach used is described in the Community Based Participatory Watershed Development Guidelines (CBPWDG). This process involves communities identifying the most appropriate technologies that respond to the unique needs of each individual watershed included in the RLP-T. The CBPWDG also provides guidance on how to prioritize and implement these solutions, including best practices as well as ensuring that expected results are achieved and sustained. More information is provided in Section F.2.

During the participatory planning communities first present the problems they have (problem analysis) and depending on the availability of labor and finance they prioritize interventions based on the Community Based Participatory Watershed Development Guidelines (CBPWDG). Once prioritized and agreed the plan is approved by various stakeholders including the community, local government, and other relevant stakeholders. The Community Based Participatory Watershed Development Guidelines, excerpts of which are provided in Annex A.2.

The objectives of this component will be achieved through the implementation of the following sub-components: (i) Land restoration and watershed management; (ii) Climate-smart agriculture; and (iii) Livelihood diversification and connections to value chains.

## Sub-component 1.1 Land Restoration and Watershed Management

Sustainable Land Management activities will be funded by IDA and MDTF in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 55.9 million) and by GCF in watersheds identified for GCF funding (budget of USD 100 million). Afforestation/Reforestation Green Corridor - Activities will be funded by IDA and MDTF only in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 7 million).

This sub-component will focus on the implementation of land rehabilitation measures and establishment of green infrastructure through biological land and water conservation measures. These measures are required primarily for the enhancement of soil fertility, productivity, and ecological services in areas where there is a high proportion of converted lands, as well as to enable and maintain agricultural production in harsh climate conditions which are exacerbated by droughts and floods. The activities will be implemented in degraded and abandoned agricultural land in the targeted micro-watersheds from increased ecological services and land productivity, mainly through productive use and conservation of natural resources. This sub-component will also introduce the establishment of green corridors, which will further reduce erosion, enhancing watershed restoration, and increase ecological connectivity.

The objective of the sub-component will be achieved through biological and physical conservation measures that ensure reduced surface run-off and soil erosion, as well as improved land productivity, resulting in enhanced crop and livestock production. The following activities will be supported:

- Soil and water conservation measures on communal or privately cultivated lands: biological and physical soil and water conservation measures/practices such as contour farming, terracing, check dams, water harvesting (e.g., damming), mulching, etc., that can be implemented in degraded communal and farmlands.


---
**Page 27**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (2:1) | PAGE 23 OF 96

B

- **Green rehabilitation:** Cost-efficient biophysical guild restoration techniques such as sandbag check dams, sediment storage dams and gully-check dams will be applied. Productive use and management of the rehabilitated land will be ensured through community participation.
- **Establishment of green corridors:** Planting suitable, preferably native, tree species along mainstreams and all-weather roads connecting forest patches in the watersheds. Post-plantation management support including protection from grazing animals, weed control, and regular monitoring to ensure that trees are established along gully offets to ensure stability and productive use of the land.
- **Area-based rehabilitation:** Rehabilitation of degraded areas through a combination of tree plantations on free grazing, enrichment planting, soil fertility improvement and moisture retention will be implemented in community areas. The establishment of green corridors will also be supported by the construction of small-scale enclosures that include supporting local communities in the preparation and execution of participatory use and management plans.
- **Establishment of plantation blocks:** Reforestation and afforestation of degraded forest and shrubland lands with a mix of native tree species to improve soil moisture retention, increase biodiversity, enhance productivity and enhance fertility of the land. Planting of appropriate tree seedlings including economically valuable species, and post-plantation management support such as protection from grazing animals, weed control, hoeing and weeding during early stages will be carried out to ensure survival of the planted seedlings; and
- **Enrichment of degraded pasture and rangeland:** Planting and reseeding of appropriate forage species including native grasses and legumes. This will increase the productivity of pastures and improve the value of feed for grazing animals. Management of unplausible invasive species will also be undertaken in pasture and rangelands to ensure optimum forage production.

Suitable rehabilitation interventions for each micro-watershed are determined based on the particular agro-ecological conditions and incorporated in a MYDP, developed through a participatory process, utilizing the technical parameters provided by the Ministry of Water and Irrigation (MoWI) and the Climate Change and Biodiversity Fund (CCBWFund), 2005) developed by MoI, and currently being updated. MYDPs already exist for SLM-II watersheds, but they will be developed under the SLMII for the watersheds that have not yet been addressed.

Supported by the Zonal, Regional and National Platforms (see details in Annex 2), implementation of MYDP is coordinated through a multi-stakeholder partnership involving the Water Resources Commission (WRC), the Water Works Corporation (WWC), the Kebele Watershed Development Committee (KWDC) and the beneficiary communities. Together with the Development Agents (DA) and Community Facilitators, the WWC and KWDC assist communities in: (a) developing a watershed management plan; (b) identifying priority areas for rehabilitation and reforestation from the Regions' work plans and budgets; (c) facilitating community participation in watershed planning and rehabilitation; (d) implementing the watershed management plan. The MYDPs will be developed by the WRC and WWC. This sub-component will also provide resources for local expertise to be contracted to provide technical assistance to WWDCs and KWDCs, as well as to train communities on watershed management practices.

The operational details for the planning, design, and implementation of MYDPs will be planned during implementation. A guideline for the development of Watershed Management Plans (WMP) and a Performance Assessment for Watershed Management (ESPMAP) - A Guideline for Sustainability" (see Annex L3).

- Pitting and planting of multi-purpose trees on degraded lands

## Sub-component 1.2 Climate Smart Agriculture

Activities will be funded by (IDA and MDTF in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 18.5 million) and by GCF in watersheds identified for GCF funding (budget of USD 0.7 million).

Interventions under this sub-component aim at enhancing the livelihood resilience of beneficiary households through Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) interventions in all eligible micro-watersheds assisted by the project. The improved agricultural practices will be designed to increase productivity and income, while also contributing with reduced degradation-related risks (achieved through sub-component 1.1), will provide suitable conditions for beneficial soil management and water conservation practices, and will contribute towards climate-smart agriculture production systems. For this, technical and financial assistance will be provided to stable soils and increasing fertility, improving the resilience of smallholder farmers.

The introduction of climate-smart agricultural technologies and productive practices in farm plots and home gardens will promote the adoption of climate-smart linkage and production practices in farm plots and home gardens. The introduction of these technologies and practices is expected to increase agricultural productivity in coming decades given expected climate change impacts as described in section 2.3.

This sub-component will build on the achievements of sub-component 1.1, such as improved water-rural retention and infiltration, guilded guano fertilization, and enhanced biomass production. This connection to the biophysical restoration of the landscape is important, as it will help ensure that unsustainable agricultural practices do not re-emerge in the future.

The CSA interventions will be designed based on a watershed-based landscape approach and contribute towards the goal of climate resilient watersheds. The ongoing soil conservation and land management activities under sub-component 1.1 will also provide the foundation for achieving this as a single measure or practice. In order to achieve the triple win of adaptation, mitigation and increased production, the CSA interventions will be designed in close collaboration with the soil conservation and land management activities.

The primary set of technologies for CSA that have been selected for use in the project are described in the manual for Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA) A3.1 (see also section 4.2 Technical Evaluation for more information). The CSA interventions described on the manual for CSA which outlines its work/capacity packages, will be supported under this sub-component:

- Farm water and soil moisture management (based on WorkActivity Package 1 of CSA manual): This will include into in-situ soil moisture management practices such as improved tillage, mudding/membrane soil cover and water harvesting. The use of appropriate farm tools/technologies for improved conservation tillage will be considered under this activity.
- Integrated soil fertility and soil health management (based on WorkActivity Package 2 of CSA manual): Various soil amendments such as organic manure, compost, biofertilizers, green manure, compost and manure management (including bio-digester), lime and gypsum application for acid and alkaline soils will be considered under this activity. The use of appropriate farm tools/technologies for crop rotation and legume intercropping will be integrated as a package and promoted based on local conditions and farmers’ needs.
- Crop development and management (based on WorkActivity Package 3 of CSA manual): Access to better performing crops (drought and disease resistant) will be supported based on local-level adaptive research

- Reduce the use of external resources for livestock feed treatment and improvement of feeding troughs.

#### Interventions:
- Implementing integrated pest disease management, including post-harvest management to minimize loss of high-value crops such as vegetables and fruits.
- Improving farm tools and machinery like line ploughs, stage and harrowing equipment will also be tested to improve the efficiency of crop production in Watershed Areas.

The alternative technologies and practices selected are:

- Soil moisture management including drainage and cutoff drains in micro-watersheds
- Agroforestry
- Diversified drought resistant crops
- Improved farm machines/tools (handheld harvester, ripper, line planter, mechanical weed slasher)
- Compost/organic fertilizer
- Improved livestock feeding troughs including feed treatment materials

The RILP promotes Climate Smart Agriculture, including the use of mulch, cover crops and minimum tillage, which also seeks to minimize the application of agrochemicals. The combination of CSA activities and the implementation of the improved crop production technologies will result in a significant reduction in the environmental impact.

The resulting improved crop production together with the provision of high-yielding and disease tolerant seeds will support efforts to minimize the use of pesticides and agrochemicals in the project area.

RILP resources will not be used to purchase pesticides, herbicides, biocides, or GMO and Patented Hybrid Seeds.


Sub-component 1.3 Livelihood Diversification and Connections to Value Chains

Activities will be funded by (IDA and MDF) in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 6 million) and by GCF in watersheds identified for GCF funding (budget of USD 20 million). Activities for the promotion of high performing cowpeas will be funded through a combination of IDA and MDF (budget of USD 1.5 million) and GCF (budget of USD 0.5 million). No GCF funds will be requested for activities related to the promotion of high performing cowpeas.

This sub-component includes innovative activities that will enhance the sustainability of the resilient, improved livelihoods in watersheds targeted by sub-components 1.2.1 and 1.2.2. The introduction of sustainable land management practices and climate smart agriculture will ensure long-term improved incomes for the vulnerable smallholder farmers targeted by the project will enable them to maintain the rehabilitated watersheds. Without the activities in this sub-component there is a risk that poverty will lead the beneficiaries back into unsustainable livelihoods, reversing the gains made through the introduction of sustainable land management and climate smart agriculture.

The activities include advisory services and investment to improve access to and implementation of climate resilient livelihood diversification. These activities will help address the issue of landless/poor women and the resulting increasing vulnerability of rural women to be more at potential risk to reduce climate risks. A number of potential beneficiaries have been identified. Examples include: extension services for smallholder farmers, development, vocational training, processing equipment and CSIRPs; facilitation of access to markets, technology and inputs; provision of seedlings and other inputs; and the establishment of a revolving fund mechanism that facilitates private sector engagement in RILP-supported value chains directly or through primary cooperatives and/or co-operatives. The revolving fund will be used for the purchase of inputs and services from the private sector.

In addition, this sub-component will provide small seed-grants funds to SHGs, CICs and/or WUs to launch or expand their businesses in climate resilient livelihood diversification activities. This is a new activity and therefore what will increase incomes of households and sustainability of the rehabilitated watersheds. Finally, the WU will be used for the purchase of inputs and services from the private sector (this is not being requested for this activity). Non-sustainable use of biomass for cooking is one of the main drivers of deforestation in the project area. The introduction of improved cookstoves that reduce demand for wood for fuel will continue, undermining progress in resilient land use made through the introduction of sustainable land management and agriculture. Reducing demand for fuelwood is critical to maintaining restored landscapes in


---
**Page 31**
---

project components. In addition, as heating and cooking-efficiency improves, use of manure and crop residues for cooking and heating decreases, allowing these materials to be used on fields to enhance soil fertility.

MoA has drafted eligibility criteria for choosing watersheds and comminoties eligible for value chain. For example, the followings are criteria for watershed selection: Sense of ownership of communities' watersheds managing team, i.e., Capacity, experience and commitment of (CIVT, KWT, WT, CRTS, WRC). Watersheds aligned with GIZ-SUPRO project, which have been rehabilitated under SUPRO project. Watersheds that have a minimum coverage area of rehabilitated land in watersheds; soil fertility that is suitable for high value commodities; availability of alternative livelihoods and income generating activities; Accessibility for market linkages and reliability of commodity supply; Accessibility to inputs provision; extension services of private sector, including the involvement of the private community and local government (BMH and FHII) to accept for new innovations, technology, etc. – Whether RLLP is providing support for CSA - Unemployment status of the communities within the watersheds.

RLLP is only for providing kind support such as warehouses and equipments and (b) organizing awareness training and workshops for various groups. No sub-loans or sub-grants will be made to any group or individual beneficiaries.

### Innovation for climate resilient livelihood diversification and private sector development

These activities will focus on private sector development (PSD) in RLLP rehabilitated community watersheds. Product processing and buying capacity will be developed that will enable sustainable, environment friendly livelihoods, thus increasing incomes, which in turn lead to the maintenance of rehabilitated watersheds and improved land productivity. The following are the activities under this component: In the absence of the private sector, newly developed livelihood interventions will diminish and eventually fail; causing households to revert to old practices and leading to further degradation of the environment and loss of ecosystem services from the watersheds, leaving them exposed to risks driven by climate change. If watershed communities produce high-quality, seasonally stored commodities for bulk purchase by the private sector, they will encourage the private sector to go "to the mill" to process and market commodities even though low-quality, unprocessed and unliced commodities might be closer at hand.

#### Processing Equipment and Training: Activities will support the shared purchases of and training on key agricultural processing equipment, which will increase the value of crops produced through climate smart agriculture (CSA) and invested in by RLLP. The following are examples of such equipment that can be purchased by watershed communities/households. Such equipment may include the following: Forage processing mills; grain threshers; weighing scales; grain mills; processing sheds, dairy processing equipment; poultry and egg processing equipment; bamboo processing equipment and tools; and vegetable storage/transport containers.

Heavy equipment is not included in the list of eligible equipment. The use and maintenance of eligible equipment is delineated in the bylaws and governance structures of CDSs, cooperatives, self-help groups, female groups to which such equipment may be made available.

#### Community Storage Receipts Programs: The project will foster the development of community storage receipts programs (CSRP) in RLLP rehabilitated watersheds by building communities or other types of storage facilities and training community organizations to develop and maintain CSRPs. One of the major barriers to the implementation of resilience building measures by farmers is lack of cash. Once the project is completed and concessional finance

### Crop and other value chain challenges

Crop and other value chains will vary according to weather with conditions with prolonged droughts, integrated pest management Community Economic Groups such as the following: white bean, green mung bean, chickpeas, soybeans, wheat, maize. The watersheds identified for interventions have been selected based on the need of the communities. Additionally, domestic demand for locally grown food has informed the selection of value chains.

### CLIMATE INCREASED RISK

The CSPF will support the establishment of diverse buffers to mitigate temperature/precipitation, frost due to climate change and related pests/diseases which might adversely impact the quality and value of the crops. These stakeholder groups will be overseen by the respective cooperative, union, or watershed user association. The CIGs will be responsible for developing the management structure of the CSPF Management. Based on their legal standing and capacity, cooperatives, unions, or watershed user associations will manage the CSPFs. Cities and Sheds are also subscribed as members to participate in CSPF schemes.

### SLMP-1 AND SLMP-2 EXPERIENCE

The CSPF-related initiative was not tested during the SLMP-1 and SLMP-2. However, the Ethiopia Agricultural Growth Program (AGP) provides lessons and experience regarding CSPF Pat has been developed for the implementation of the CSPF in Ethiopia. The AGP website (http://www.eacm.com.et) have also generated relevant experience and capacity in SLMP-I and SLMP-II supported watersheds

The cooperatives/watershed user associations and the CIGs will be responsible for developing the management and business structure of the CSPF. Primary management responsibilities will be held by cooperatives or watershed user associations, while the CIGs will be responsible for providing technical support to farmers when designing their bylaws and marketing strategy. The CSPF supports farmers to overcome their immediate production challenges through a combination of inputs, training, and access to markets subject to fluctuating prices within the harvest and lean seasons. The System is an important and effective tool for creating liquidity in the farming community during the lean season, improving food security, increasing income from the market, improving smallholder farmers' incomes, and reducing food losses. The system can play dominant role in the development of a resilient agricultural sector by providing access to markets at more equitable terms within each season, allowing them to access markets on more equitable terms, and enhancing the efficiency of the entire commodity chain. The CSPF has legal personality and can enter into agreements with the farmers.

There will be a strong emphasis on the formation, strengthening of and support to activities of the CGIIs under this sub-component because these semi-formal groups, which may transition to either primary cooperatives or enterprise structures, are governed by MUA-approved guidelines, including requirements for organization, planning and financing. This sub-component will provide resources for local expertise to be contracted to provide technical assistance to support beneficiaries in establishing CGIIs, as well as in implementing income-generating activities and investments to strengthen connections to local value chains.

In the co-financed support of livelihood diversification, emphasis will be given to the establishment of CGI/SiGs/PGs for the production and marketing of improved cookstoves. These groups not only provide an alternative source of income, but also contribute to improving household food security by reducing fuelwood collection and achieving health improvements through reduced household air pollution, and reduced pressure on local biomass resources through improved household energy efficiency. As heating and cooking efficiency improves, use of manure and crop residues for cooking and heating decreases, allowing these materials to be used on fields to enhance soil fertility.

Initial support for livelihood diversification and connections to value chains will target beneficiaries in watersheds that have already been or completed implementation of their MUPs, where support for CSA is being provided and support for CGI/SiGs/PGs has not yet been initiated. The CGI/SiGs/PGs will be established at the watershed level to facilitate success at this pilot level and any for needed adjustments before scaling up these activities in later years of the project. In addition to Sincas and CGIIs at the watershed and/or watersheds level, stakeholders involved in this sub-component include: (1) the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Irrigation and Cooperatives (MIALC) at national levels; the Rural Saving and Credit Associations (RuSaGACs), private sector enterprises and their sectoral associations, including the National Association of Cookstove Producers (ANAPCO); the Agroforestry and Forest Agreements for Sustainable Development (AFADES); the Office of Cooperation Promotion (OP); support organization of CGI/SiGs/PGs to produce energy-efficient cookstoves; the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (MARN) at national level, and other local level gatherings; (2) through the Bureau of Energy, Water and Mineral, provide technical expertise to conduct assessments of household energy options in RLP intervention areas and support beneficiaries/consumers support in establishing local channels of finance (such as traditional savings groups - e.g.).

Potential maladaptation risks with initial commodity processing are mitigated through the design of the project. The project promotes sustainable land management on all land targeting to the larger watersheds. Hence, even if agricultural production expands as a result of the development of markets for commodities, the expanded production will not be at the expense of forest cover and biodiversity. In addition, the expansion in agricultural production on lands already in use will increase substantially, leading to a decrease in the need for new agricultural land. Land that is currently under cultivation but has been abandoned due to lack of water or other factors will prevent uncontrolled expansion of agricultural land, as only those with land certificates will be eligible to participate in the rural land regularization process (RLP). The RLP will also provide incentives for the establishment of tree cover (which are already highly deforested and degraded). Furthermore, the establishment of green corridors and elimination of firewood collection areas will contribute to the creation of a mosaic landscape with enhanced forest cover and preventing deforestation. RLP will support SLM practices to limit free grazing in project areas including the use of improved livestock breeds, as well as the promotion of agroforestry systems and integrated agri-silvo-pastoral practices. The creation of information platforms and provision of technical assistance will also reduce the risk of maladaptation, as information and outreach will result in increased awareness and improvements in local livelihoods.

### Component 2: Investing in Institutions and Information for Resilience

The objective of this component is to enhance institutional capacity and improve information for better decision-making in supporting resilient landscapes and diversified rural livelihoods in the project area, both for the duration of the project and after project completion.

This component will build capacity at the local government level (woreda and kebele) for:

- Planning and managing SLRM activities
- Developing and implementing a monitoring system to track progress towards achieving the project’s objectives
- Using information technologies for information modernization at the local level, including the use of electronic tablets for gathering geopositional information, and the use of unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) or drones for land certification mapping. Tablets and UAVs will be used exclusively only by the project. The woreda-level team will train and support the woreda focal persons in the project watersheds for mapping and monitoring.
- The device setup, training, and support provided to the woreda focal person will include: a laptop, a tablet, a drone, a camera, GPS, maps, tools, accessories (protective case, solar charger, etc.), guidance materials, technical and trouble-shooting support.

RLFP intends to monitor all households using UAVs. The current capacity within the PCU is classified as low to moderate for implementing RLF activities. For this reason, RLFP will not be able to provide sufficient staff for data management duties. The use of UAVs in Ethiopia is currently limited due to GUE security concerns and the lack of a regulatory framework. Therefore, RLFP proposes to work with UVA (a private company) to develop guidelines on the use of UAVs that would ultimately support monitoring efforts more broadly and with fewer restrictions. This will be done by providing training for the woreda focal person in the use of UAVs and related activities as demonstrated by receiving permission to use UAVs to collect imagery for parcel demarcation under the land certification project. The data collected using the UAV will be stored on a secure server, where all data are collected and analyzed with additional expertise to manage the UAV monitoring component.

Support for policy development under this component will focus on the regulatory framework for Watershed User Associations (WUA), community bylaws guiding land-use practices, and strengthening the Land Administration System (LAS) in Ethiopia. This will be achieved through a series of workshops and training sessions leading to project completion. To strengthen the evidence base for sustainable land management decision-making, this component will include a bio-physical impact evaluation of SLWM interventions, to be conducted through a partnership arrangement between CGIAR’s MoA, WLE, and the Ethiopian Environment and Forest Research Institute (EERI) and Ethiopia Development Research Institute's Environment and Climate Research Center. This will complement a livelihoods assessment that is being undertaken by EERI in collaboration with CGIAR’s MoA and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). The results from these assessments, along with lessons learned from the pilot sites, will be shared through workshops and training sessions for key stakeholders such as local communities, research institutions, and Government agencies.

This component's objectives will be achieved through the implementation of the following sub-components: (i) capacity building, information modernization and policy development; and (ii) impact evaluation, knowledge management and communication.

Sub-component 2.1 Capacity Building, Information Modernization and Policy Development

Activities will be funded by GEF and MDFT in watersheds identified for GEF funding (budget of USD 7.8 million) and by GCF in watersheds identified for GCF funding (budget of USD 16.5 million).

This sub-component will build capacity at local government level to implement RILLP, and to sustain SLWM interventions after watershed graduation from project-based support. To achieve this, the sub-component will finance accounts to support the head of the Watershed Office of Agriculture (WUA) and a focal person in each participating watershed office. The sub-component will also provide technical assistance for capacity building at the major watersheds. To help build the capacity necessary for an effective land administration system, this sub-component will also provide technical assistance for training in this field.

This sub-component will support information modernization to coordinate data collection and information sharing at all levels and under components of the project so that this information is well organized, properly documented and accessible. The use of electronic tablets (e.g., Android) or other mobile devices to collect data on the field created during the course of RILLP will be documented, stored and otherwise managed. The use of electronic tablets to collect data in the field will reduce the time required for data collection and improve accuracy. The use of software will improve the quality and timeliness of data collection and induce the need effort to compile, review, and generate regular summary reports. This framework will facilitate access to information and support timely feedback to the local level.

This sub-component further supports the use of aerial vehicles (UVAs/UAVs) to generate high-quality and timely aerial imagery data to support planning, monitoring, and land certification. Under this initiative, the drones will be operated by several teams of trained operators who will travel to the project sites. During the course of RILLP each micro-watershed will have at least one set of images taken from a drone. The images will be processed into high-quality images of the program areas. At each stage the processed imagery will be shared with the watershed and local field staff for their review, feedback and use in planning and monitoring activities. A comprehensive inventory of tools and materials produced will also be used to support M&E and will serve as a source of information and data for subsequent projects. The sub-component will cover all costs associated with the procurement of drones, including rental requirements for the drones, all associated equipment and spare parts, operating costs for the duration of the project. The use of the drones is intended for the collection of information and data that will be available for long-


---
**Page 36**
---

term use for project planning and monitoring. The project will work with the Information Network Security Agency (INSA) and the Ethiopian Aviation Authority to ensure all necessary permits are obtained.

Policy development under this sub-component will focus on the regulatory framework required for the establishment of WULAs, including the formulation of a policy document that will guide the implementation of the project in regard to reward and incentive schemes such as Payments for Environmental Services (PES), as well as community byelaws guiding land-use practices, and strengthening of the Land Administration System.

In developing the framework for WULAs, The Project will work closely with regional governments for its application in establishing WULAs. This work will commence with reviewing of the environmental legislation that relates to the use and management of water resources in Ethiopia. In addition, a review of existing policies and strategies related to the use of CCAFS will be used to promote and enhance this activity. RILP will pay high attention to the opportunities of engaging smallholder farmers in the WULAs through the implementation of the project. The second objective is to develop an enabling environment for the private sector investment in RLP, to attract the PS in invest in RLP interventions. The second objective is to create and increase income streams from the use of water resources by smallholder farmers, particularly women through the promotion of inclusive business and value chain/partnership relationship based on profitability principles.

Sub-component 2.2 Impact Evaluation, Knowledge Management and Communication

Activities will be funded by IDA and USAID in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 5.1 million). No GEF funding is available to support this sub-component.

Impact evaluations (IEs) will use rigorous research methods to look at specific interventions under RLP, assess the contribution of these to development goals and provide robust evidence of SLIM impact. Project funding will focus on the following areas: i) The evaluation of the bio-physical impacts of the project; ii) The evaluation of the social and economic impacts of the project; iii) The evaluation of the institutional impacts of the project. The evaluation is expected to be led by the Gender Innovation Lab of the World Bank’s Africa Region, financed separately. The bio-physically relevant indicators will include soil moisture content, vegetation cover, land use change, water quality parameters such as peak and base surface water flows, groundwater levels and recharge rates, sediment loads, and evapotranspiration. The social-economic indicators will be related to the project’s impact on livelihoods. In addition, we expect the extending partnership between MiA, the Water and Land Resource Center of Addis Ababa University (WALRU), and the Ethiopian Institute of Agricultural Research (EIAR) to continue with the support of relevant international research organizations. IEs are expected to be completed within the Implementation Period (5 years) and the MiA will provide service providers for such purposes. The IE’s disclosure will be subject to WEF’s access to information policies.

In addition to the bio-physical IE, and the livelihoods IE an evaluation of climate-smart agriculture will also be conducted. This evaluation is expected to be completed within 5 years. The evaluation methodology is currently being developed and will be further elaborated in the terms of reference, acceptable to the World Bank. Basic design of the IE’s is expected to be as follows: In the first stage a stratified selection of watersheds will be performed; and in the second stage watersheds will be paired with a suitable comparison watershed (outside project watershed) based on similar characteristics such as rainfall, soil type, land use, etc. The evaluation methodology will be elaborated with each watershed monitored. The CCA evaluation is expected to follow a treatment-control-comparison methodology. In addition, the evaluation of the MiA’s climate-smart micro-watershed is being explored. In any case, the sampling of treatment and control will be randomized.

To build a solid and effective knowledge management system both for this project and the SLIM program in Ethiopia, this sub-component will establish a geospatial knowledge platform that combines information from a variety of project and other sources and packages it in a format that is accessible to planners and stakeholders at the national, regional, and local levels. This activity will build upon the work being done by WLEA under SLIM II to

The media content will be developed using a mix of qualitative/quantitative research methods (FGDs, community level meetings, surveys).
- **ii.c.** Public awareness raising activities on land tenure issues, including land use planning procedures and conflict resolution activities on land use planning, to explain the benefits of (formalized) rentals and unlock the blockage set by cultural norms, emphasizing that temporary land renting does not imply abandonment and formalized rental contracts do not result in land being appropriated.

Activities are co-funded by IDA and MDTF in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 26.5 million). No GCF funding is requested for this component.

As indicated under the root causes section above, land tenure insecurity caused by frequent land redistribution in the past has encouraged farmers in Ethiopia to favor short-term exploitation of land resources over long-term conservation, contributing to land degradation and declining productivity. The objective of this component will be to strengthen the capacity of communities to manage their natural resources sustainably through secure land rights and access to invest sustainably in productive landscapes. This component will be funded entirely by co-financing. No funding from GCF is being requested for this component. Refer to detailed budget (Annex A, 1).

Component 3 will provide security of tenure to smallholder farmers through Second Level Landholding Certification (SLLC) as an incentive to increase the adoption of SLLC technologies and practices. The on-going SLLC exercise at various levels in Ethiopia is a good example of how this can be achieved. This component will also support the use of drones for orthophoto production and para-surveyors for field level data acquisition, and data encoders for office level data management. Other activities include: (i) establishment of an SLLC database; (ii) development of a drone-based aerial mapping and mobile mapping using tables as applicable. This component will also extend the on-going local-level participatory land-use planning exercise at the kebele level within the framework of the National Land Use Plan. The project will support consultation workshops for land-use plan development at the kebele level and to connect these consultations to the larger land-use planning exercises underway at the regional and national levels.

This component will also support the rollout of the NRLAIS in BLP woredas that do not overlap with other land administration support projects. In Gambella, the project will support the installation and roll out of the NRLAIS both at the regional and kebele level. The NRLAIS will be installed on a server-based system hosted by the MoA which will provide security, transparency, maintenance of the land information with enhanced data management functionality. At the kebele level, the NRLAIS will be installed in the Woreda Land Administration Unit (WLAU) and will also equip the regional and federal authorities with an adequate tool to produce and avail statistical data on rural and tenure security issues that facilitate evidence-based monitoring and ensure a coordinated and consistent approach to the development of policies, legislations, standards, models and measures to enhance sound land governance across the country.

At the woreda and kebele levels, implementation of this component will be undertaken jointly by the Woreda Office of Land Administration and Use (WLAU) through the Kebele Administration Office, the Kebele Land Administration Unit (KLAU), and the Kebele Land Users Association (KLUA). At the woreda level, a team of 10 field teams will be contracted, trained and deployed, each comprising a team leader, a para-surveyor, a data recorder, a digital tablet, and a laptop. The field teams will be responsible for the installation and roll out of the NRLAIS certification activities. Woreda and kebele land use teams will also prepare the preparation of Participatory Local Land Use Plans. At the Regional and Zonal levels, the Bureau of Land Administration and Use (BoLAU) and related agencies will be responsible for the installation and roll out of the NRLAIS at these levels.
