## Synergies with interventions on land administration support from other development partners have been identified.

These include the Responsible and Innovative Land Administration in Ethiopia Project ("REILA") being implemented by Finland and the Land Investment for Transformation Project (“LIFT”) being implemented by the UK Department for International Development (DFID). The REILA project is a 5-year project with a total budget of €7.81 million. The project aims to: (a) improve regional land administration; (b) increase certified land tenure security for land users in six regions in Benishangul-Gumuz and Addis Ababa; and (c) reduce the number of land disputes by 50% in the six regions.

## LIFT is a four-region project (Oromia, Amhara, SNNPR, Tigray) with a total budget of €23 million. LIFT aims to support the Government of Ethiopia’s efforts to improve the efficiency and productivity through the development of rural land markets and its supporting operations.

In 2015, the Ethiopian government signed an agreement to enter into a land community partnership with the Federal Republic of Germany to promote joint rural land governance for economic growth and to protect the rights of local communities. The project will support the implementation of this agreement by providing technical assistance in the areas of land administration and cadastral surveying, as well as capacity building and collaboration among the Government of Ethiopia and its development partners.

Since then the GIZ Land Partnership has been an active partner in several projects managed under GIZ portfolio such as SLURP (GPV) and LURG (TA).

## Activities will be funded by IDA and MDFTP in watersheds identified for IDA funding (budget of USD 11.5 million) and by GCF in watersheds identified for GCF funding (budget of USD 6.09 million).

The objective of this component is to effectively consolidate plans, budget, implement and report on project activities with due diligence and integrity.

This component will finance the operationalization of Regional Project Coordination Units (RPCUs) in Mek'ele and Regional Project Coordination Units (RPCUs) in Amhara. There will be 7 RPCUs—one in Addis Ababa and one in each of the regions in which the project will be implemented (Amhara, Oromia, Tigray, Benishangul-Gumuz, and Gambella). These RPCUs will carry out activities related to procurement management, human resources management, social and social safeguards, Monitoring & Evaluation (M&E), sector coordination of investment targeting and policy harmonization, and donor coordination structure. The project will support a modernized M&E system that includes the development of an integrated monitoring and evaluation framework and a standardized data collection that incorporates tools for capturing spatially explicit activities and area treated will be reported in a timely manner to the GCF.

The RPCUs will also enhance the capacity of the Mek'ele RPCU to manage the improved M&E system component 2.1. The enhancements and functionality incorporated into the M&E system will improve the quality, accuracy, timeliness, and reliability of data for reporting and providing feedback on local land management progress which will support improved decision-making.

## Scale of the project, identification of targeted project area and beneficiaries

In terms of the scale of the project, the World Bank is confident that the benefits of implementing the interventions included in the project at the relatively large scale proposed outweigh the risks. A number of factors mitigate these risks, key among them being the fact that the project builds on experience gained by the World Bank and the Federal Government of Canada (FGC) from their previous investments in the region. The project will build upon the lessons learned from the benefits from the projects aimed at sustainable land management, poverty reduction, and climate change adaptation implemented under the FLCF and other Canadian-funded projects.

These lessons led to the creation of the institutions that RLP will build upon such as bottom-up watershed planning and participatory water resource management. The project will also draw on the experience gained from previous generations of activities are combined to achieve the triple goals of adaptation, mitigation and livelihood development.

For the Executing Entity, RLP activities will come on top of activities with a budget of $316 million that are already spent or committed for SLMP which are managed or coordinated by MoA. For both the sums already spent and committed and for co-financing the World Bank provides to RLP, the World Bank has conducted risk analysis and identified the risks associated with each project. The lessons learned from these projects will be applied in this project.

The valuable experience gained during implementation of SLMP as well as the significant Recipient Experience (RE) gained by MoA through its participation in the FLCF and other Canadian-funded projects are expected to be instrumental to improve or identify viable measures to address the risks.

### Total needs were a major consideration in deciding on the scale of the proposed project. Soil degradation is an ongoing problem that is becoming more severe with every passing year. There are significant costs related to inaction – the longer we wait to address the problem, the worse it will get, and the more expensive it will be. To achieve the aims of the project, the project will work with communities that use these landscapes. Hence, beneficiaries are selected at the community level. The project will target 210 major watersheds in the region. These include 57 watersheds already targeted by SLMP and FLCF. In these watersheds RLP will implement only innovative climate resilient activities that were not included in SLMP and FLCF. For GCF financing, 40 out of 126 watersheds identified as vulnerable to climate change will be supported.

In order to achieve the aims of the project – achieving restored, productive and low emission landscapes, the project will work with the communities that are using these landscapes. ## Detailed Project Institutional and Implementation Arrangements

The organizational structure and arrangements acceptable to the World Bank for the implementation of the recently completed SLMIP will be maintained and streamlined for the execution of RILP. Implementation will be carried out at four levels: Federal, Regional (including Zonal), Woreda (district) and Kabele (sub-district). and decisions in the meetings are subject to MoA concurrence.

The National SLM Steering Committee, chaired by the State Minister responsible for Natural Resources Management in MoA, comprises high-level representation from MoF, MoWIE, MoFECC and DPL. The Steering Committee is responsible for: (1) approving the annual work plan; (2) providing overall supervision and general oversight for project implementation; (3) reviewing and approving the consolidated annual work plan, budget and financial report; (4) reviewing and approving the project implementation performance report; and overseeing the execution of any corrective actions that may be designed.

The National SLM Technical Committee is also chaired by the State Minister responsible for Natural Resources Management in MoA. It is made up of senior technical staff from institutions such as MoA, MoWIE, MoF, MoFECC, MoWCA Ministry of Women and Children Affairs, the Ethiopian Institute for Agricultural Research (EIAR), Cooperatives Union, and representatives from non-governmental organizations actively engaged in SLM activities. Generally, this body is responsible for: (1) providing technical advice on the quality of implementation performance reports; (2) providing technical advice on the quality of implementation performance reports and special studies such as policy and legislative drafts, financial and audit reports, documentation of best practices, and MoA reports.

The SLMIP Project Coordination Unit (PCU) at MoA, which is staffed by 30 technical and fiduciary staff, will continue to play the role of managing and facilitating the day-to-day implementation of the project. Specific tasks will include: (1) coordinating with the National SLM Steering Committee and Technical Committee; (2) ensuring the implementation of work plans and corrective actions, safeguards instruments including management/institutional plans; (3) preparing progress reports on the implementation of the work plan and corrective actions, as well as other safeguard instruments (e.g. management/institutional plans); and evaluating project impacts; and (4) preparing progress reports for the National SLM Steering Committee and Technical Committee. The PCU will be staffed by competent and financial management specialists, MoA expert and technical experts in diverse disciplines (including watershed management, soil erosion control, land use planning, knowledge management & communication, livelihoods, private sector development).

**Regional and local level**

Implementation of activities on the ground is supported by, among others, Regional steering and technical committees. The Woreda implementation entities, the BoD will review and consolidate annual work plans, budgets, procurement plans submitted by the Woredas. The Woredas will be responsible for implementing activities on a day-to-day basis. Together with the BoD, they will form a regional Project Coordination Unit for each of the six Regions in which the project will be implemented (including Amhara, Oromia, Tigray, SNNP). Beneficiaries will include farmers and other stakeholders who are directly involved in agricultural production. The Woredas will provide technical support, extension services and M&E to a group of Woredes under its jurisdiction. The ZACs will coordinate with the Woreda for the implementation of activities. The BoD will review and consolidate annual work plans submitted by entities to participate in the implementation of activities, draw lessons from the project and support scaling up of SLU practices. The BoD will also provide technical guidance and training on M&E, financial management capacities, and advise for specific outputs (such as preparation of MYDPs and WWPUs, establishment of WUA, and preparation of business plans for GDA and value chain linkages) in 20 zones where RILP will be implemented.

#### Woreda and Kebele levels

On-the-ground planning and execution of activities under the project will be undertaken jointly by WGA, the Kebele Water Users Association (KWUA), and the Kebele Land Administration and Use Committee (KLAUC). The KWUA is recruited at woreda level to improve financial management capacities and reduce implementation risk. Thus, WAs, KWAs and KLAUCs will be responsible for: (i) developing annual work plans and budgets; (ii) preparing project plans for submission to the BoD for review and endorsement and integration into a Region’s annual work development plans and budgets; (iii) facilitating and mobilizing community participation in watershed planning and implementation of activities, including land use planning and management, water resource management, etc.; (iv) extension service delivery and dissemination of best-of-technologies and innovations, etc. Implementation of Community Watershed Management Plans will be supported by the Woreda Water Resources Development Administration Offices, the Kebele Land Administration and Use Committee (KLAUC), the land administration and use DAs and the communities.

## Roles and responsibilities of the World Bank

The World Bank as the Accredited Entity of the project will play an important role in programme supervision and implementation. The WB will ensure that the RLP is executed in line with the WB policies and procedures. The WB’s role in the management of the RLP is described at length below, including a discussion detail, including a discussion document, is provided in section F-4 Financial Management and Procurement.

## Financial management

Payments will be made upon an approved annual work plan and budget. To ensure transparency as well as to enhance the level of disbursement under the RLP, quarterly Interim Financial Reports (IFRs) follow international reporting standards and are submitted promptly at the end of each quarter.

An external audit is conducted by a recognized independent auditor such as the World Bank Institute auditor or an accredited private audit firm. The audit will be conducted in accordance with Terms of Reference prepared by the EE and the objective of the audit is to ensure that the RLP is being implemented in line with the approved work plan. The WB is responsible for overseeing and providing a no objection for the recruitment of the auditor including no objection of the TOT. The WB will verify that the audit is conducted in accordance with the International Standards on Auditing and that the auditor has taken into account the findings issued; if necessary, the WB will issue corrective actions throughout the execution of the RLP.

The GCF Proceeds will be channeled through the World Bank and will be made available to the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia. The World Bank will enter into a grant agreement and a loan agreement with Ethiopia, representing the FGE, as the recipient country. The World Bank will also enter into an implementation agreement with MoA responsible for overall project implementation and accountable for the Funded Activity’s outcome indicators.

MoA will be working closely with MoF, the Ministry of Environment, Forest and Climate Change (MEFCC), the Ministry of Health (MOH) and other relevant government agencies. The World Bank will provide technical assistance as required to ensure that the RLP is implemented in line with the project supervision plan, monitoring, evaluative review, reporting, and technical assistance activities.

## Procurement

As the AE, the World Bank is responsible for ensuring that MoA has the necessary procurement capacity required for the RLP. To this end, the WB has conducted a procurement capacity and risk assessment of MoA (see Annex L1). The WB will be responsible for ensuring that procurement under the project will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank’s policies.

### In terms of the requirement for GCF funding, there are two types of interventions in this project:

The first type of intervention involves scaling up demonstrated measures for SLM. In past activities, Wb and the GIZ have laid the foundations for sustainable agricultural production and improvement of livelihoods. SLMIP I and SLMIP II, which were implemented by the Ethiopian government with support from Wb and GIZ respectively, have been learned for further improvement of activities in the future.

With over 10 million hectares of degraded land under rehabilitation, most of them average farm sizes between 5.5 ha and 2 hectares, the agricultural sector does not yet have the means to fund the introduction of SLM on all degraded watersheds without concessional funding. The Ethiopian government is investing heavily in climate change adaptation and mitigation activities. In 2017/2018, the total budget for climate change adaptation was 35 billion birr (of which around 40% ($6.4 billion) was from within the federal budget of the Ministry of Agriculture). Of the regional budget, the largest share is allocated to water resources development and management. The government has also increased spending ($2.8 bn) on protecting the most vulnerable people in society through a program of safety nets that provides food assistance for those who are unable to access sufficient food due to climate change. Given the ongoing expected in Ethiopia and the vulnerability of most of the population, this investment will not be sufficient and GCF funding is required to fully finance the incremental costs of climate change adaptation.

The government of Ethiopia has been investing successfully in the development of SLM. SLM practices address both the short-term (erosion control, flood control) and the long-term goals of the government, which are part of efforts to improve food security and livelihoods for rural communities. However, the current level of public resources is insufficient to fund the remaining SLM investments required and additional funding is needed to finance the required activities. The Ethiopian government has been able to access concessional financing from the World Bank through concessional IDA credit. The loans requested from GCF for these investments are at a similar level of concessionality as those provided by the World Bank. This is in line with the GCF policy on concessional lending to Least Developed Country with a GDP per capita of $710 or less in 2017. In addition to SLM investments, GCF funding will also be used for other climate change adaptation activities that are part of the overall strategy and budget of SLM activities. This risk includes the limited human resources to support beneficiaries in the planning and

## Implementation of complex interventions, the challenge of implementing a cost-effective M&E system, and the need to strengthen coordination among institutions, sectors, programs and projects.

The GCF highly concessional funding, along with additional financing from DAC, MDTF and GoE, would build upon previous SLM practices, taking into account lessons learned and introducing new activities in order to achieve landscape restoration and establish green corridors. Activities would include land rehabilitation, intercropping, the adoption of agroforestry systems, and the establishment of buffer zones around water bodies and other critical ecosystems.

Large-scale landscape restoration is only achievable through GCF co-financing due to the nature and scale of the interventions required. This will also provide a strong foundation for increased resilience to climate change and mitigation capacity while it enables agricultural production.

The second type of intervention for which GCF funding is requested is that of measures intended to encourage the adoption of sustainable land management practices, such as agroforestry systems, intercropping with livestock based on SLM and CSA. By strengthening value chains linking livestock-based on SLM and CSA practices, this will provide a strong foundation for increased resilience to climate change and mitigation capacity while it enables agricultural production.

If correctly implemented, CSA helps increase yields while building farmer resilience and contributing to the achievement of the NDCs and several SDGs. Thus, CSA jointly addresses food security and climate change adaptation and mitigation. The determining factors for effective CSA outcomes are the combination of practices such as agroforestry systems, intercropping with livestock based on SLM and CSA, and the adoption of CSA by farmers. The implementation of this combination of practices, such as the need for a change of mindset of farmers, extension services and other stakeholders is critical to the success of these interventions.

Concessional funding is needed in order to remove these barriers and create a culture and knowledge base within which CSA can continue to be promoted by the external services and implemented by farmers in future.

Without GCF involvement, Ethiopia cannot finance the proposed interventions. The national Climate Resilient Green Economy strategy has called for annual spending of $7 billion to respond to climate change with national budgetary constraints. In addition, the country is heavily dependent on international aid, with development and international sources contributing tens of millions of dollars per year, there is a major financing gap. Poor access to credit, high transaction costs, and lack of awareness among farmers about CSA are some of the barriers hindering local climate change impacts. In addition, Ethiopia's Debt Sustainability Assessment recently changed the risk of debt driven by climate change from low to medium. A significant increase in the price of concessionally is needed to ensure improved resilience to climate change impacts and food security in Ethiopia.

Public goods include: management of communal land; externalities from soil erosion; and water-macrosy (risk of droughts and floods). Market failures are found in the incomplete markets associated with land-macrosy (risk of defined land ownership); water-inscurity; and soil loss. Improving management of non-land areas under communal land use requires a public good approach. Livestock grazing on communal lands is not possible without addressing soil erosion and soil erosion on these communal lands (e.g., non-cropland). Using private investments to improve resource management on communal lands is not possible unless all costs and benefits are internalized. The only way to ensure that the full range of public goods associated with land management is one approach to this, but it requires long-term public investments and capacity building beyond what the government can afford.

Soil erosion may lead to impacts outside the water-managed area (externalities). This means that costs and benefits from the investment will be accrued by people outside the project area. There is no functioning market for externalities in Ethiopia; therefore, addressing these with private sector investments or public-private sector investment is required.

### Explanation: The project will seek to ensure that the long-term maintenance of restored landscapes through (i) an emphasis on strengthening the value chains associated with sustainable agricultural practices in restored watersheds designed to be climate-resilient and productive; (ii) a policy implementation support for the establishment of carbon credits, certificates, to encourage investment in long-term landscape productivity; and (iii) policy an implementation support for the establishment of a regulatory framework for the promotion of SLM. The project will also seek to create a durable institutional framework SLM. For value chain connections including CSRP – these investments will be made inside projects and/or through public-private partnerships, with the aim of covering all costs and the revenues necessary to cover them, that will be generated through the connections to value chains.

GCF funding will be used to enhance the climate resilience of and add innovative elements to the government's ongoing SLM programs. Strong government ownership ensures long-term commitment to the promotion of SLM and CCA practices, as part of the broader national goals of enhancing agricultural productivity, building resilience to climate change, and improving food security. The GCF will support the Government in scaling up and enhancing the success of the Government’s project flagship SLIM Program. Beyond this national commitment, a particular focus is on ensuring that SLM interventions are implemented at scale within the government's state units for SLM, such that maintenance of restored landscapes and CCA can become mainstreamed into local community practices. The GCF will support the Government in developing a regulatory framework for SLM, which will be a capacity that will enable the CSA interventions to be sustainably implemented in watersheds that graduate from project-based support. Spillover effects of successful SLM interventions have already been observed under the ongoing government program, and these spillovers are expected to continue as the Government extends its efforts into adjacent areas and replicated through the government extension services. In addition to this spillover effect, the RILP will provide four specific forms of support for the graduation of watersheds:

- First, the principal emphasis of policy development under RILP will be the establishment of a regulatory framework that will ensure the long-term maintenance of restored landscapes and CCA practices in restored watersheds. This initiative will build on a pioneering effort in the Regional State of Amhara and will draw on international best practice in this regard. In addition to providing the institutional framework required for maintaining SLM, the RILP will also provide policy support for the development of new regulatory instruments designed to leverage possible new sources of funding for SLM. ## B

### Environmental Services (PES), such as payments for sustainable watershed management to deliver downstream benefits, for example by reducing flooding and sediment loads affecting hydrological infrastructure, will be supported through the RLP. This includes, inter alia, as well as payments for groundwater recharge from private sector entities depending on reliable water supply.

- **Second**, support for capacity building and information modernization under RLP will emphasize building people’s capacities to manage their resources in a sustainable manner.
- **Third**, the focus of RLP on strengthening value chains associated with livelihoods based on SLM and CSA practices, as well as promoting agroforestry systems, will be expanded. This will help farmers to expand these initiatives.

Finally, financial support for land-holding certification will help secure land tenure for smallholders, enhancing income opportunities and promoting resilient livelihoods in the long term. Land tenure provides incentives for sustainable land management, to abandon destructive practices such as free grazing, and to persevere with CSA practices.

### Measures that will be taken to enhance institutional capacity for implementation and sustainability are:
- **i)** continual training on project management and monitoring at all levels, in coordination with the GIZ SURED project.
- **ii)** project implementation arrangements acceptable to the World Bank and agreed by the MoA and regional governments playing a role in capacity and targets at all levels; and
- **iii)** coordination between development partners’ Technical Committee on SLM.

### Sustainability will be ensured through the creation of exit strategies for each participating watershed, based on the guidance provided in Annex 1 of the ESAPMII (2013) and the Water Management Plan (WMP) (Annex 4), which also covers operation and maintenance (O&M). Annex 7 of the ESAPMII provides a sample framework for a watershed management plan. The WMP is an important tool to guide the development of watershed management projects in Ethiopia. This framework includes the development of an O&M plan for all infrastructure financed by the project, including water storage facilities and other infrastructure such as irrigation and livestock facilities, which have not been considered as part of watershed development plans in the past, and consequently not covered under the existing WMPs. The WMP also provides a framework for the management of springs/halite wells, guarding, user fees, community-level trainings for O&M and O&M of introduced crop farming.

### By the end of the project period, all watersheds included in the project are expected to have completed a Multi-Year Development Plan (MYDP) and those already supported under SLM-P will receive assistance to graduate from project-based support. Watershed Management Associations (WMAs) will be established at watershed level with support for the creation of Watershed User Associations (WUAs) in each graduating watershed to replace the project-based support. Watershed Water Users’ Committees (WWUCs) and Watershed Knowledge Owners’ Committees (WKOCs) with a legally recognized institution for the ongoing planning and management of the watershed.

### Watershed Management and Use Plans (WMUPs) adopted by WMAs will detail management and use for graduating watersheds, outlining agreements to conserve and utilize the resources and establishing rules for managing and implementing conservation activities and the distribution of benefits. The development of these WMUPs is expected to be completed in 2018. This will enhance adaptive capacity to climate change, promoting resilience broadly at the landscape level.

### Ongoing monitoring of the success of SLM and CSA will be ensured at the local level through the RLP’s support for information modernization as part of local government capacity building. At the national level, the involvement of MoA and national research organizations in the impact evaluation, knowledge management and communication

### Component 2. Investing in Institutions and Information for Resilience
Activities will be funded by IDA and MDTF in watershed identifiers for GCF funding (budget of USD 13.0 million) and by GCF in non-watershed development areas.

The objectives of this component are to enhance institutional capacity and improve information for better decision-making in supporting resilient landscapes and diversified rural livelihoods in the project area, both for the duration of the project and after project completion.

This component will build capacity at all the local government level (woeda) and tables (i.e. Planning and managing SLIM watersheds) to support the implementation of the land certification process. This will include piloting of new technologies for information modernization at the local level, including the use of electronic tablets for gathering geospatial data on woedas and tables. The woeda and table boundaries will be defined based on the existing administrative boundaries of woedas and tables in the project area (i.e., MoWa) and would be provided to development agents and the woeda local government. The pilot activities will include the use of electronic tablets for gathering geospatial data, which will be tailored to meet the conditions and realities faced in field environment (i.e., off-line data collection), accessories such as solar chargers, and other necessary equipment.

Support for policy development within this component will focus on the regulatory framework for Watershed User Associations (WUAs), community bylaws guiding land-use practices, and strengthening the Land Administration System (LAS) to ensure that all information is captured in a timely manner. This will be completed at project completion. To strengthen the evidence base for sustainable land management decision-making, this component will include capacity building of woeda officials on the use of SLIM tools and other relevant technologies. A joint development between the MoW, the Water and Land Resource Centre of Addis Ababa University, and the Ethiopia Development Research Institute (EDRI) will be established to provide technical assistance for the implementation of the modernization of SLIM interventions. The pilot activities in this component will include the use of electronic tablets for gathering geospatial data on woedas and tables.

This component will also provide resources to manage the knowledge generated through the project, including local governments and communities, relevant research institutions and Government agencies, as well as Development Partners.

The above objectives will be achieved through the implementation of the following sub-components: (i) capacity building, information modernization and policy development; and (ii) impact evaluation, knowledge management and communication.

#### Sub-component 2.1. Capacity Building, Information Modernization and Policy Development

This sub-component will build capacity at local government level to implement RELIP, and to sustain SLIM interventions in the project area. The GCF funding amount for this sub-component is USD 3 million. This component will finance accountants to support the head of the Woeda Office of Agriculture (WoOA) and a focal person in each participating woeda to ensure that all information on land use, land cover, and other relevant data are captured at the local level (i.e., watershed). To help build the capacity necessary for an effective land administration system, this sub-component will also provide technical assistance for WoOAs in the project area.

This sub-component will support information modernization to coordinate data collection and information sharing at all levels of government. As part of this effort, a data management plan will be developed that specifies how data are used or created (e.g., land use maps, soil erosion maps), who is responsible for creating and maintaining the data, and what edits to collect information on project activities and results, combined with appropriate survey and mapping software.


---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 53 OF 96

This sub-component further supports the use of aerial vehicles (UVAs) drones to generate high-quality and timely aerial imagery data to support planning, monitoring, and land certification. Under this initiative, the drones will be operated by several teams of trained operators who will travel to the project sites. During the course of RLP each micro-project area will be flown at least once with a drone. The drones will be equipped with cameras that capture images of the project areas. At each stage the processed imagery will be shared with the worlds and local communities, and materials produced will also be used to support MAE and will serve as a source of information and data for the development of WULAs. This will include the creation of maps and other products such as orthophotos. The technical requirements for the drones, all associated equipment and spare parts, operating costs for the duration of the project, and the training of the operators will be covered by the GCF. The drone imagery data will also be used to support project planning and monitoring activities including the Environmental Security Network Agency (ESNA) and the Environmental Authority. Authority to ensure that necessary permits are available.

Policy development under this sub-component will focus on the regulatory requirement for the establishment of WULAs, as well as the development of a national policy framework for the implementation of such policies and incentive schemes such as Payments for Environmental Services (PES), as well as community bylaws guiding land use and management. The project will also work with relevant stakeholders to develop a national strategy.

In developing the framework for WULAs, this Project will work closely with regional governments for its application in establishing WULAs. This work will commence with reviewing of the environmental legislation that relates to the use and protection of water resources in Ethiopia. The findings from this review will be used as inputs into the CCAW will be used to proceed and enhance this activity, RLP will give high attention to the opportunities of engagement of private sector actors in WULAs establishment and management. This is expected to increase the capacity to attract the PS to invest in RLIP interventions. The second objective is to create and increase income streams by diversifying the sources of revenue for water resources management through the promotion of inclusive business and value chain/partnership relationship based on profitability principles.

### Sub-component 2.3 Impact Evaluation, Knowledge Management and Communication

Impact evaluations (IEA) will use rigorous research methods to look at specific interventions under RLIP, assess the contribution of these to development goals and provide robust evidence of SLIM project. Impact funding will focus on the evaluation of the impact of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) and water management interventions. The approach to be led by the Gender Innovation Lab of the World Bank’s Africa Region, financed separately. The bio-physical impact evaluations will be conducted in two stages. In the first stage, a baseline survey will be carried out for each watershed peak and base surface water flows, groundwater levels and recharge rates, sediment loads, and remotely sensed information on land cover and use. This will be followed by an endline survey after five years of implementation. The existing partnership between MoK, the Water and Land Resource Center at Addis Ababa University, and the Environmental Science and Research Centre of EDF, and aim to build new partnerships with national/international research organizations.

In addition to the bio-physical IEA and the livelihoods IEA an evaluation of climate-smart agriculture will be conducted in collaboration with the CGIAR Climate Change Initiative (CCCI) and the Ethiopian Agricultural Development Research Institute (EARDI). The basic design of the IEA is expected as to be follows: the livelihoods IEA is expected to involve random assignment. The bio-physical impact evaluation will be carried out by a third party independent consultant. A baseline survey will be performed, and in the second stage watersheds will be paired with a suitable comparison watershed (outside project area) for each of the 10 watersheds. This will allow for the identification of changes that are associated with each watershed monitored. The CSA evaluation is expected to follow a treatment-control comparison methodology where the control group will be the same as in the CCA micro-watersheds being explored. In any case, the sampling of treatment and control will be randomized.

To build a solid and effective knowledge management system both for the project and the SLIM program in Ethiopia, this sub-component will work with the Ethiopian Agricultural Development Research Institute (EARDI) to compile all project and other sources and packages it in a format that is accessible to planners and stakeholders at the national, regional, and local levels. This activity will build upon the work being done by WLRUC under SLIMP to develop a web-

A strategic communication program will be developed and implemented under this sub-component to inform and mobilize communities, enhance project visibility and transparency among all actors, support efforts to scale-up G.M.L.M and CCA activities in the region, and ensure that lessons learned are captured and disseminated. As part of the implementation of the Knowledge Management and Communication (KMC) program have been developed following a rapid KMC needs assessment conducted by the project team. The KMC program will be implemented through a combination of communication with effective channels, techniques, tools and key messages that address the communication and knowledge management needs identified in the needs assessment. By implementing this program, and by helping those guidelines, implementers will have room to elaborate, modify and adapt additional communication and knowledge management activities as needed.

The specific objectives of the KMC program are: a) Support scaling up efforts for adoption of G.M.L.M and CCA practices through awareness raising campaigns; b) Strengthen the capacity of local institutions and coordination of monitoring and evaluation; c) Enhance the program visibility among all actors involved in the project, including farmers, extension workers, government officials and other key stakeholders.

The KMC program will be implemented as a stand-alone component. The identified overarching goals are 1) to build and coordinate a strong knowledge base contributing to the effective implementation of G.M.L.M and CCA practices; and 2) to ensure that lessons learned from the project supported activities. The specific objectives of the KMC program are: a) Support scaling up efforts for adoption of G.M.L.M and CCA practices through awareness raising campaigns; b) Strengthen the capacity of local institutions and coordination of monitoring and evaluation; c) Enhance the program visibility among all actors involved in the project, including farmers, extension workers, government officials and other key stakeholders.

### Possible activities include:

- **i.** Knowledge identification, capturing, validation and packaging annually to support scaling up efforts, build capacity of user groups, youth groups, DAs and FICs (experiential knowledge, best practice and synthesis of expert knowledge, lessons learned, etc.), and other key initiatives for RLP effective implementation (e.g., organization of Stakeholders Workshops)
- **ii.** Awareness raising campaigns through the following: a) Using FICs as facilitators to organize at local level and delivering contents into areas in need; b) Outdoor activities such as road shows, audio and video materials to be used as supporting tools during workshops and events, and media tours for journalists and PR officers of relevant bureaus to show progress.
- **iii.** Knowledge sharing networking events (e.g., annual SLM Knowledge fair); and
- **iv.** Activities related to the development of a communication strategy and other key initiatives for RLP effective implementation (e.g., organization of Stakeholders Workshops)

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section aims to evaluate the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's initial Investment Framework.

### D.1. Expected Performance (500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the potential of the project/programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas. As applicable, describe the envisaged project/programme impact for mitigation and/or adaptation. Provide the expected performance against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's initial Investment Framework.

### D.2. Expected Performance (500 words)

Describe how the project/programme contributes to sustainable development pathways. Provide the impact of adaptation by describing on how the project/programme contributes to building resilience and reducing vulnerability, including climate change impacts. The description should be consistent with section E.2 Reporting GCF’s core indicators.

In terms of the requirement for GCF funding, there are two types of interventions in this project. The first type of intervention is related to the adoption of sustainable land management (SLM) practices and adaptation. Between 2007 and 2013, the government's total investment in agriculture was around $1.1 billion, of which $584 million was spent on SLM activities. In addition, the government has invested $69.5 million from its general budget ($0.3 bn) was spent on resilience activities related to addressing key climate risks. Around 80% of current national investments in agriculture are directed towards SLM and adaptation. The second type of intervention is related to providing income support and social assistance. However, due to the significant impacts of climate change especially on smallholder farmers with average farm sizes between 0.5 and 2 hectares, the agricultural sector does not have the means to fund the introduction of SLM in all degraded areas.

With over 95% of agriculture output generated by smallholder farmers with average farm sizes between 0.5 and 2 hectares, the agricultural sector does not have the means to fund the introduction of SLM in all degraded areas. The government has been investing successfully in the development of the SLM practices among both the above-mentioned groups of farmers. However, there are some efforts that are being made to rehabilitate degraded areas through soil and water conservation measures. However, national resources are insufficient for this purpose. In addition, the government has not yet developed a comprehensive strategy to ensure interventions in degraded watersheds. To date, the World Bank has supported these interventions through concessional loans and grants. The GCF is expected to provide additional funding to support SLM practices, especially as the IDA Credit. Highly concessional funding is appropriate due to Ethiopia's status as a Least Developed Country with a high poverty rate. However, it should be noted that highly concessional funding increases the risk and overcomes the barrier of limited capacity to scale up the current coverage of SLM activities. This risk includes the introduction of new technologies and practices, which may not be sustainable in the long term without the implementation of a cost-effective M&E system, and the need to strengthen coordination among institutions, sectoral policies, and stakeholders.

The GCF highly concessional funding, along with additional financing from IDA, MDGTF, and GEF, would build upon previous SLM practices, taking into account lessons learned and introducing new activities in order to achieve sustainable development. The project/programme will focus on the following areas: land restoration, soil conservation, low tillage, guild recultivation, establishing grazing corridors, water points and wells, and silvo-pastoral practices. Large-scale investments are required for these interventions, which may not be possible without the needed investments. Land restoration lays the foundations for increased resilience to climate change and mitigation capacity while it enables agricultural production.

The second type of intervention for which GCF funding is requested is in a list of measures intended to encourage the adoption of SLM practices by smallholder farmers and the development of strong value chains associated with livelihoods based on SLM and CSA. By strengthening value chains linking livelihoods based on SLM and CSA practices, the project/programme will contribute to increased resilience and adaptation capacity for smallholder livelihoods, providing incentives for maintaining SLM and CSA practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 55 OF 96

Without GCF investment, Ethiopia cannot finance the proposed interventions. The national Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) Program estimates that to achieve its goals, it will require $30 billion in budgetary resources for climate change relevant actions estimated to be in the order of $40 million per year and interest-free concessional financing from GCF. Without such funding, Ethiopia’s ability to access credit, high lending rates and an insufficient budget are not conducive to the investments required for handling local climate risks. The lack of investment will result in a further decline in agricultural productivity, increased food distress to households, and higher vulnerability to climate change impacts.

Without the Project intervention, beneficiaries both in the area and downstream will continue to struggle to establish or maintain their livelihoods due to soil erosion, water scarcity, and land degradation. Continued soil erosion, water scarcity, and land insecurity leads to land degradation with direct losses to those that rely on it for food security and income generation. The lack of access to credit, high interest rates, and low fertilizer prices will mean that farmers will have to increase their input costs, e.g., fertilizer, to maintain current yields. In the absence of storage facilities, this will result in a further decline in agricultural productivity and increased food distress for households.

The analysis assumes a declining production due to soil erosion with Project intervention, but with Project implementation, there is an expected yield increase over time. This yield increase is attributed to adoption of improved cultivars, employment of conservation agriculture techniques (e.g., crop rotation, minimum tillage, crop residue management and crop intercropping), and the use of fertilizers. D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the degree to which the proposed activity can catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment:

- Potential for scaling up and replication
- Potential for knowledge sharing and learning
- Contribution towards achieving national environment
- Overall contribution to climate resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change

The RLP will scale up, transform and innovate through the government of Ethiopia's ongoing SLM program. Following the success of earlier SLM interventions, the RLP represents a paradigm shift by focusing on building the institutional framework for SLM in degraded watersheds. The RLP is based on the premise that communities are both resilient to climate change and sequester carbon. Transformative elements of the RLP include (a) an emphasis on strengthening local institutions and governance, (b) a focus on community-based land management and engagement to build incentives for local communities to maintain restored landscapes over long-term periods; (c) a focus on the provision of technical assistance and capacity building at all levels of government; and (d) a focus on providing necessary support for the establishment of watershed associations, combined with capacity building of local governments, to ensure their sustainability.

The theory of change behind this package of interventions (as shown in the illustration below) is that by delivering more productive, secure and resilient livelihoods to local communities and by establishing the institutional framework needed to sustain these benefits over time, the RLP will lead to a significant increase in SLM adoption at scale. At the local government level, the RLP will lead to a durable shift towards SLM in the degraded watersheds of the Ethiopian Highlands.

Such dissemination will be encouraged through awareness generating initiatives and training programs undertaken under Component 2.1, including farmer-to-farmer experience exchange visits, field schools, and awareness raising workshops. The RLP will also build on the strong existing relationships with key stakeholders and local communities in the project watersheds, who have already contributed immensely to effective Project implementation. These include: (a) religious leaders; (b) community-based organizations; (c) traditional knowledge institutions such as (Sido), "Yehager Shimagawedas", (Edens), religious fathers, "Mahabber", etc.), which have been established and are active in the project areas; (d) local government officials at all levels of government; and (e) dissemination of SLM practices (refer to Annex D.1 RLP Social Assessment). In addition, Ethiopian communities are also involved through their participation in savings groups and credit associations (RODCA) called eedaa rather than through the underdeveloped formal financial sector.

RLP will be implemented by MoA, the ministry responsible for agriculture in the entire country. The ministry, working together with its implementing partners, will ensure that the RLP is fully integrated into the national SLM strategy and RLP will expand the project area further (See Figure 2). The Government of Ethiopia aims to introduce sustainable land management practices on an estimated 10 million hectares by 2030. In order to scale up existing SLM programs, there is every intention to continue the process of scaling up in the coming years. MoA and MoF are committed to scaling-up and ensuring the long-term sustainability of the Government's proven flagship SLM Program.

RLP will also seek to identify innovative sources of SLM financing, including Payment for Ecosystem Services (PES) schemes, such as those being implemented by the Ethiopian Forest Service in collaboration with the Ethiopian Wildlife and Natural Heritage Organization (EWNHO), and other public-private partnerships. RLP will also work closely with Rayna Breeyew-BB Ethiopia in the Tigray Region, or on public sources such as municipalities and River Basin Authorities. The RLP will also seek to identify opportunities for co-financing through existing national physical and biological infrastructure, including for hydropower, irrigation and water supply. Further information on the project's engagement with the private sector is provided in Annex D.2.

The knowledge generated and experience gained through implementation and evaluation of RLP will be disseminated more broadly to inform the design of SLM interventions internationally.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 57 OF 96

## Environmental Benefits

Benefits from improved water management include increased soil moisture and reduced variability in response to flooding and drought, and irrigation provides benefits both in terms of net soil quality and off-site terms of reduced erosion; it can be measured in terms of land savings or erosion prevention. Increased soil fertility is a determinant for the productivity of crops and livestock, which in turn contributes to food security, poverty reduction and improved downstream water quality, while simultaneously supporting biodiversity, which will be further enhanced through agroforestry.

An illustration of the benefits that sustainable land management can provide in Ethiopia is provided by the Productive Safety Net Program (PSNP). Under RLP, a number of communities graduating from food-eucracy status in newly identified PSNP areas are now able to produce enough surplus food for sale. This has led to increased forest restoration and sustainable land management and mitigates nearly 24 million CO2 per year (>100%), achieved by sequestering carbon in biomass and soils. This equates to 1.5% of Ethiopia's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to mitigation.

## Social and Economic Benefits

The principal direct effect of this RLP will be improved income and more resilient livelihoods of vulnerable households on degraded watersheds targeted by the project as a result of investments in SLM, climate resistant livestock diversification (crops, meat, dairy, and beekeeping), tree-feeding small ruminants, and agroforestry. These activities are expected to provide alternative sources of income for alternatives for PES or CCBs; value addition opportunities will be created through the sale of these products. The activities are also expected to diversify livelihoods in a number of ways: (i) health benefits of reduced exposure to harmful air pollutants and improved water quality, (ii) reduction in malaria transmission due to increased use of insecticide-treated or impregnated cookstoves, and (iii) enhanced livelihood resilience as a result of reducing flooding and sediment loads. Benefits from improved administration and tenure rights include conservation of protected areas, biodiversity and tourism.

RLP will support climate resilient food security of communities graduating from the PSNP and prevent a loss to food insecurity of these communities as a result of climate shocks, resulting in social and economic benefits for vulnerable households. The project is expected to reduce poverty by 10% (from 45% to 35%) among people who were met by improving land restoration and infrastructure, and smallholder farmers increased maize yields by an average of 20%.

Project funded capacity building and institutional development at all levels have direct value in that they increase the skill level in public sector institutions and enable them to work more efficiently in providing essential and enhanced public services. The project will also provide training for extension workers on climate change adaptation (CCA) and Sustainable Farming Approaches (SFA), but they are seen as critical to ensuring that the other benefits can be realized when it comes to building productive capacity of smallholder farmers.

According to a financial and economic analysis (FEA) of the RLP, the estimated value of avoided emission varies between US$ 0.1 and US$ 3.0 tonnes of CO2 depending on the gross marginal land use value (USD $11/tonne of land). The FEA also shows that the project will generate a net benefit to farmers in terms of increased income. Financial and Economic Analysis conducted during project preparation estimates a farm-level gross margins increase of more than 50% for maize, sorghum, and teff; this is equivalent to an increase from USD $120 per ha (current Food Poverty Line). When assuming 5 persons per household farm, the gross margin can increase to at least USD 100 per person per year. This is more than double the current income of a typical rural household above the National Poverty Line for Ethiopia. The poverty line indicates the money required to afford the food covering the minimum regulated caloric intake (Food Poverty Line) and additional non-food items. The improvement in farm gross

# Gender Sensitive Development Impact

In addition to promoting women’s participation in water-user committees, the RLP will also experience other gender-sensitive development impacts as women fully share in project benefits. In particular, women will continue to be specifically targeted in the issuance of land-holding certificates, and in the design of support for income-generating activities, such as the provision of seeds, tools, and training on improved agricultural practices and kitchen gardens and cookstoves. To ensure that gender-specific lessons are learnt during implementation of the RLP, a socio-economic impact assessment will be conducted by the World Bank’s Africa Region Gender Innovation Lab. Specific gender-sensitive development impacts include:

- **Strengthened implementation practices (planning, implementation and monitoring processes) for equitable inclusion of women in all aspects of project activities**, including sustainable land restoration and water conservation practices (50 % female representation in all stages).

- **Integrated landscape management practices adopted by local communities based on practical and strategic gender needs and priorities**.

The SLMP has produced several gender-related benefits. For example, it had a positive influence on gender norms and increased women’s economic generation through the SLMP was appreciated by women participants, who pointed out that they have gained more respect from community members because of their increased self-reliance. Women also reported that they are now better informed about family planning and health issues as well as about women's roles and capacity, and women have started to feel more confident and motivated to engage in IGAs and climate change adaptation activities.

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the project’s needs in detail, including those that are not directly addressed by the project/programme. Identify any gaps or deficiencies in the project/programme that could hinder country progress and/or address the issue (e.g., the level of exposure to climate risks for beneficiary country and groups, overall vulnerability of the country). Describe how the project/programme addresses these needs.

- **Vulnerability of the country**: Ethiopia is highly vulnerable to climate change. The most common gender-related vulnerabilities include lack of access to land, lack of education, lack of economic opportunities (including gender-specific roles), lack of decision-making power, and lack of awareness.
- **Economic and social development level of the country and the affected population**: Ethiopia’s Second National Communication identified the primary causes of vulnerability to climate variability and change as poverty, low levels of human capital formation, poor infrastructure, weak institutional structures, and lack of awareness. The most cited causes include under-development of water resources, low health service coverage, a high population growth rate, lack of access to credit facilities, inadequate road infrastructure in drought-prone areas, weak institutional structures, and lack of awareness.
- **Access to services**: The country is highly dependent on the performance of the agriculture and forestry sectors, which are highly sensitive to climate change. Climate change will have significant impacts on the productivity of these sectors, including a decline in the performance of productive landscapes for income, energy, food, building materials, and water. Furthermore, changes in rainfall patterns may lead to increased soil erosion and more frequent droughts and floods.

According to the vulnerability assessment in the SNC based on existing information and assessments, the most vulnerable sectors to climate variability and change are agriculture, water and human health. In terms of livelihoods, smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they depend heavily on rainfall for their crops and livestock. The agricultural sector is highly dependent on the performance of the agriculture and forestry sectors, which are highly sensitive to climate change. Climate change will have significant impacts on the productivity of these sectors, including a decline in the performance of productive landscapes for income, energy, food, building materials, and water. Furthermore, changes in rainfall patterns may lead to increased soil erosion and more frequent droughts and floods. The arid, semi-arid and dry sub-humid parts of the country are affected most by drought.

Furthermore, some project regions exhibit low adaptive capacity, which increases vulnerability. Although it is not possible to have an exhaustive list of indicators that assess adaptive capacity of a region due availability of processed data, there are several factors that can be used as proxies for assessing adaptive capacity such as quality of water, energy and settlement, and indirectly to the level and quality of education and health facilities. For example, the use of modern construction materials directly indicates the quality of settlements available to resist the physical impacts of climate change. Similarly, the availability of well-equipped and functional health facilities are indicators of poorer quality, or that lower levels of supplementation and vaccinations are being provided.

The adaptive capacity indicators above indicate that most of the targeted watersheds are situated in regions that have low levels of access to piped water and electricity, which is an important indicator for assessing adaptive capacity. These areas, which are characterized by high poverty levels, are comparatively more vulnerable to climate change than other regions in the country. Institutional capacity was also assessed as a proxy for adaptive capacity. The study identified several institutional capacity issues in the Gambia region of the country. It identified three critically important weaknesses, including a lack of institutional policy, awareness or understanding of institutional capacity.

In conclusion, this study examines the potential impact of climate and climate policies on poverty reduction. It suggests that as a result of differences in exposure and vulnerability, natural disasters increase inequality and may contribute to the persistence of poverty. The study also shows that the Gambia region has experienced a drought that lasted over 10 years. This drought has had a decade-on average for asset poor households to bring livestock holdings back to pre-famine levels. Poor people can often only afford to buy food at high prices, which increases their vulnerability and makes them more vulnerable in times of need, better access of poor farmers to markets, and improved technologies and climate-smart production techniques are needed to help these communities adapt to the impacts of climate change. For example, in Ethiopia, the incidence of poverty decreased by 6.7% following farmers’ access to all-weather roads. Case studies show that providing resources for climate adaptation can be a key factor in reducing vulnerability from zero to about $50 per household if support is delayed by four months and up to $13,000 if support is delayed by six months. The study concludes that the most effective way of supporting poor households (especially livestock), helps explain why most post-disaster responses have multiple stages. Typically, initial support is delivered in a short-term emergency phase, followed by longer-term recovery and development phases. Support provided later with more emphasis on appropriate targeting. The authors conclude that providing resources for climate risk management can be an important element of poverty reduction strategies. This is particularly true when the development and poverty reduction investments can provide a window of opportunity before the impacts of climate change become irreversible.

## Figure 4 Population density in Ethiopia

By focusing on the most degraded watersheds in the Ethiopian highlands, the RILWP will target the communities most vulnerable to climate change impacts. Under Component 1, sustainable soil and water conservation practices will reduce exposure to climate-related impacts such as erosion and drought. Climate-smart agricultural practices will reduce vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change by increasing their resilience and reducing sensitivity of communities to impacts affecting the agricultural sector. Under Component 2, the capacity building and information sharing activities will be designed in a way that they are directly linked with the implementation of the RILWP. In addition, the project will support the development of policies, legislation, regulations, models and research to enhance sustainable land governance.

The project will work with the most vulnerable populations in the target areas. Detailed bio-physical information will be used to prepare MTDPs for new watersheds. Local-level participatory land use planning teams at the woreda and kebele levels will be established to ensure that the RILWP is implemented in a way that it benefits all stakeholders, particularly targeting the particularly vulnerable group of landless and jobless youth and women. In these activities, landless youth will be given priority for training on sustainable land management practices.

The project will support the development of policies, legislation, regulations, models and research to enhance sustainable land governance. The provision of landholding certification will be implemented in such a way that half of title-holders will be women. This will ensure greater participation of women in agricultural production, as well as on the agricultural market, thus enhancing their income opportunities.

## Baseline projects

The proposed project has been requested by the government of Ethiopia to both scale up the success of the ongoing SLM I and II programs, which have shown significant improvements in land degradation and soil fertility restoration caused by erosion, burning, loss of vegetation cover, and unsustainable grazing and cultivation practices. This has led to the development of a new program called the Sustainable Land Management Program (SLM). The SLM project is an unsuccessful "top-down" approach to one that recognizes the importance of community participation in decision-making, not simply as a source of labor.

The Government developed, with support from the Tewolde partnership, the Ethiopian Strategic Investment Framework (ESIF), which has included the establishment of the GEF's programmatic approach to scaling up SLM. Called the SLIM Program, it provided the platform for convening and coordinating assistance from donors. When it was developed in 2013, the ESIF was still under development, so a more programmatic approach was undertaken by the GEF and partners; efforts to address land degradation were piecemeal and scattered throughout Ethiopia.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND UNG FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 63 OF 96

As part of the SLMP Program, the World Bank/OECD-financed SLUMP-I operation targeted 35 watersheds initially, later expanding to 45. The initial target group was an estimated 500,000 beneficiaries, representing rural households living in a 12.9 million ha area of Ethiopia. The project area was divided into three major regions: (i) the central region located in six Regional States of Ethiopia (Amhara, Oromia, Tigray, SNNPR, Benishangul/Gumuz, and Gambella); (ii) the western region covering the Western Shewa Zone of Addis Ababa City; and (iii) the eastern region comprising the Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock Resource, MoAL), regional (Woredas) and district (Kebetes) levels benefited from training and improvement activities. The project was effective in March 2009 and closed on scheduled 4.5 years later (September 2013) with no extensions.

Key conclusions of the final evaluation of SLMP-I were that the project's objectives were substantially relevant to the country context and priorities. As part of the project, 45 participatory Watershed Management Plans and 613 community-based watershed management plans were developed and implemented in targeted watersheds. The number of households participating in the targeted watersheds increased from 89,602 to 209,959 by project close. The Normalized Difference Vegetation Index (NDVI) for the project area increased from 0.317 to 0.402 between 2009 and 2013. Projected areas lost by 0.54% (over a baseline of 2.48%) and were restored by 31% during this period. The project achieved an average Return on Investment (ROI) of 10-17%, with a Project ROI of 6-11%. The cost benefit analysis conducted at closure calculated an IRR that ranged from 10.41% to 25.80%.

SLMP-II was successfully considered for the GCF, which committed to a larger follow-on project, SLUMP-II, aimed at consolidating the SLMP platform and expand the number of large watersheds assisted from 45 to 135 in SLUMP-MoAL continued to develop and implement the innovative, integrated and inclusive SLMP Program that supports (i) efforts to address poverty and food insecurity; (ii) efforts to improve water security and enhance livelihoods through improved land use practices; (iii) contributes to growth in the agricultural sector in general. SLMP-II aimed at (i) further scaling up and consolidating the project's innovative, integrated and inclusive approach for watershed management in Ethiopia, particularly in large watersheds; (ii) contributing to the consolidation and harmonization of MoAL's multi-donor SLMP program; and (iii) synergizing with other GCF projects. The project was implemented through a combination of direct implementation by promoting a complementary livelihood improvement strategy anchored on "climate-smart" agricultural practices in beneficiary households.

In SLUMP-II, natural and economic wealth was built on over 1.3 million hectares of degraded communal and smallholder lands through an integrated package of activities in targeted watersheds that included: (i) management of natural resources; (ii) promotion of climate-resilient livelihoods; (iii) promoting the use of improved livestock breeds, reduced free grazing and allow assisted natural regeneration, small-scale irrigation, water point development, climate-smart agriculture practices, and agroforestry; (iv) providing access to markets for agricultural products through the sale of legal landholding certificates to one million people, including women and landless youth; and, (v) livelihoods support, including provision of improved cattle care that reduces fodder demand, women's labor, and respiratory illnesses.

Results from SLUMP-II financing are well documented. During a major drought in 2015-16 there is some evidence that water availability has increased for livestock and human consumption. The project has brought back traditional crops for local farmers, dry season base flow of streams and depth to water table are improving. In the first year of implementation, approximately 9 million tons of additional CO2eq have been accumulated in restored productive lands in SLUMP-II watersheds. Farmers report that they now have more time to engage in other activities because they regularly express how their identity and sense of place has also been restored through landscape restoration and improved livelihoods. In the first year, 10% of households reported having enough food for consumption; 25% were able to afford to send their children to school. They were able to improve nutrition by producing vegetables and fruits using agroforestry practices, increasing poultry production, and increasing livestock productivity through forage management.

Linkages with other government programs and projects

Flagship programs of the MoAL include the Second Agricultural Growth Program (AGP) and the Productive Safety Net Program (PSNP). PSNP is aimed at enabling the rural poor facing chronic food insecurity to resist shocks, create assets and become food self-sufficient. It provides multi-annual predictable transfers, as food, cash or a combination of both.

## National Strategies

Ethiopia's long-term goal is to ensure that climate change adaptation and mitigation are fully mainstreamed into development planning, policies, programs, and projects across all sectors of the economy. Some of the key objectives of the CRGE, which will be achieved through the implementation of the SLM and SLMP, include: increasing agricultural productivity and income; reducing poverty levels by increasing farmers' incomes while reducing emissions; and protecting and establishing forests for their economic and environmental benefits.

The project will contribute to Ethiopia's efforts in achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the National Determined Contribution (NDC), the 2017 National Adaptation Plan to Address Climate Change, the Ethiopia SLM Investment Strategy, the Ethiopia Forest Sector Strategy and National REDD+ strategy; as well as sector strategies for water, energy, and agriculture.

The CRGE Strategy aims at developing a green economy and promoting greater resilience to climate change into a single policy framework in support of its national development objectives. Some of the key objectives of the CRGE, which will be achieved through the implementation of the SLM and SLMP, include: increasing agricultural productivity and income; reducing poverty levels by increasing farmers' incomes while reducing emissions; and protecting and establishing forests for their economic and environmental benefits.

The project will also contribute to the Green and Transformation Plan (GTP-2), the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) Strategy, the Ethiopia SLM Investment Strategy, the Ethiopia Forest Sector Strategy and National REDD+ strategy as well as sector strategies for water, energy, and agriculture.

# Capacity of Accredited Entities and Executing Entities

Project financing will flow through MoA, which is mandated to mobilize both domestic and external resources for the implementation of the Climate Resilient Green Economy (CREGE) Strategy. The Project will be implemented by Federal Ministry of Agriculture (MoA).

Significant progress in rehabilitating degraded lands has been achieved in recent years by the Government of Ethiopia and thousands of local communities largely through investment and technical assistance under MoA’s SLIM Program. This has been made possible, among other things, due to the significant scaling up of climate-smart investments from major donors and partners (IDA, Norway, Canada, Germany, GEF, DFCD) into a holistic and coordinated landscape management framework. With financing from IDA through the SLIM, over 1.3 million hectares of degraded lands have been rehabilitated in Ethiopia’s six regional states. This has led to significant improvements in soil fertility, water retention capacity, and biodiversity, while also contributing to sustainable source of natural and economic wealth through an integrated package of activities. Working through Regional Bureaus and District Agriculture Offices, the SLIM Program has restored productivity in more than two million hectares of degraded watersheds in six regional states.

## Narrative and rationale for the detailed economic and financial analysis

To assess the ex-ante efficiency of the project investment, a cost benefit model is used. Annual cost and benefit flows are estimated as the difference between without-project and with-project net benefits for direct beneficiaries (See Annex B.1). The model uses the Net Present Value (NPV) and the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR), as well as impact on farm productivity, household income, and poverty reduction.

### Cost Benefit Model

The cost benefit model is based on a comparison between the costs associated with the project and its benefits to direct beneficiaries. The model uses the Net Present Value (NPV) and the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR), as well as impact on farm productivity, household income, and poverty reduction.

### Cost Benefit Analysis

The cost benefit analysis is based on a comparison between the costs associated with the project and its benefits to direct beneficiaries. The model uses the Net Present Value (NPV) and the Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR), as well as impact on farm productivity, household income, and poverty reduction.

### Cost Benefit Model
