also affect areas and households through increased flood risk and sediment build-up in irrigation and hydropower dams.

Incremental benefits are estimated for investments in green infrastructure and resilient livelihoods (Component I). It is assumed that the project will reduce the vulnerability of the rural poor to climate change impacts by investing in: 2. Strengthening institutions, information and monitoring for resilience; 3. Land administration and use; and 4. Project management and institutional capacity building. The incremental benefits are estimated at US$19 million from MDFT, and US$12 million from expected MEFDT Contribution from the Government of Canada for a total of US$31.2 million.

Following World Bank guidelines, the economic analysis considers anticipated costs and benefits with and without the project, including social costs and benefits. This necessitates the consideration of funding sources and labor costs related to the project. The incremental cost is estimated at US$20.8 million from MDFT, and US$13.5 million in development management totaling USD 23 million (US$13 million from GIZ and USD 10 million from the GoG). In addition, the analysis includes social costs of US$4.6 million and benefits of US$7.8 million. The total incremental cost is estimated at $39.5 million in price continuities. With all costs included, the total budget included in the analysis is USD 312.9 million.

As part of the project, the World Bank will provide a grant of US$40 million to the Government of Ethiopia and an estimated to be 2.7% of initial costs, including beneficiary-in-kind contributions of US$6.4 million per year.

The Project will increase climate resilience in 210 major waterbodies encompassing areas of over 2 million ha. Based on a 2007 census, the project area is estimated to be home to approximately 358,000 households and 986,000 people dependent on waterbodies. Since population growth since 2007 census is estimated to be 15% or more, for the present day this is a conservative estimate.

Project interventions are assumed to lead to direct net benefits to crop and livestock producers as well as forests and other non-crops through watershed management plans. These activities will reduce soil erosion and yield losses that are currently occurring in the project area. The project will also increase water availability, improve land productivity and increase resilience against the negative impacts of climate change. To further increase resilience and enhance ecosystem services, the project will include a component on forest and green cover restoration (through community groups including CSIRPs). Project activities will also constitute a net carbon sink when analyzing impact on GHG emissions. The project will be implemented through watershed management plans, land use planning, and improving information and monitoring systems. Improved administration and secure tenure rights will create incentives for the adoption of improved practices by farmers and other stakeholders. This is expected to have positive impact on indirect beneficiaries in neighboring areas through informal dissemination of new management practices as well as an increase in ecosystem services.

In the current 25-year net benefit analysis using a 5% discount rate, the project yields an Economic NPV of US$3,312 million (US$679.2 billion) and has a benefit cost ratio of 4:1. The Economic IRRI is 47%. The payback period for the project is estimated at 8 years. This analysis assumes that the project will yield an average rate of return higher than the economic discount rate of 5%. Note, that a 25-year model is used to account for the long-term benefits and costs associated with forest and green cover restoration. The payback period is based on a 20 year capitalization phase for forest plantations and green corridors.

World Bank guidelines recommend using a 5% economic discount rate. Increasing the discount rate from 5% to 10%, results in an Economic NPV of US$3,169 million (US$647 billion) and has a benefit cost ratio of 3:1. Project returns are also acceptable at a 12% discount rate with a BCR of 3:1.

If the Project only reaches half of its targets for example due to unexpected increased costs, estimated project returns would be reduced by US$50 million (US$98 billion) and the ratio of net benefits to cost would fall to 27%.

In base case assumptions too conservative or climate change leads to accelerated soil erosion in the future, the estimate of benefits of Project interventions would likely increase. Assuming a 50% increase in annual soil loss for year 30: The estimated economic NPV is US$4,189 million (US$827 billion) with a BCR of 6:1. If climate change leads to increased erosion, the estimate of benefits would likely decrease. Assuming a higher soil erosion rate than the assumption across years 25-30 in the base case, estimated value of net benefits is US$94 million (US$18 billion) and 0.26 benefit per year dependent on climate change scenario. In the scenario with accelerated soil loss, the estimate of net benefits would be reduced from US$17.0 to $3.8(billion) and BCR falls to 0.5.

**Page 72**
---

When excluding the social value of reduced GHG emissions, the net economic project return is USD 2,238 million (USD 67.7 billion) with a benefit cost ratio of 2.9, an ERR of 20% and a payback period of 7.3 years. This is 15 times that of Ethiopia’s GDP.

When excluding the GHG emissions, 46% of incremental net benefits are generated through activities on non-cropland areas, particularly due to the transformation of 41,000 ha from bush and grassland to forest plantation but also due to increased livestock production. This results in a total project return of USD 25 billion (USD 783 million) with an ERR of 43%. A substantial part of Project return is also generated by cropland and livestock production at USD 49 billion and USD 106 billion respectively. The project will generate significant benefits for the environment, including the creation of 30,000 ha of improved land to green corridor plantations and some is from avoided soil erosion. With exacerbated problems such as climate change, deforestation, desertification, water scarcity, and population pressure, this project has ecological connectivity as well as will extend the lifespan and resilience of drainage, irrigation, and road infrastructure.

In financial terms the NPV is USD 596 million (USD 18.5 billion) with a Financial IRR of 28%, a benefit cost ratio of 2 and a payback period of 7 years. The project has a high level of financial viability as it can be financed at market rates. In the financial analysis a 12% discount rate is used to reflect the opportunity cost of capital in Ethiopia.

By supporting the establishment of financially viable enterprises in the area, the Project helps build resilience and future income for the communities involved. The project will also provide additional storage capacity that is economically viable to also cover future capital maintenance costs, but only if a variable commercial loan interest rate below 10% can be obtained. This would require an increase in the size of the loans provided by the Bank. However, such investments may be available at this rate but not the size of loans required. It can be expected that demand for additional irrigation water will continue to grow and that the project will need to provide additional storage capacity. Financial viability to an IRR over 24% for example by using some of their more available storage capacity, obtaining a matching commercial loan at below 10%, or by increasing the size of loans provided by the Bank.

The Project also requires support projects to cover their initial investment costs in the absence of commercial loans at favorable rates. As such, if the project is not viable, this increased level of revenue would also enable them to cover assumed future capital maintenance costs.


The National Poverty Line for Ethiopia is a measure of absolute poverty. The poverty line index indicates the money required per day to meet basic needs (National Poverty Line). The Project will reduce poverty by 10% in terms of income. In the financial analysis, estimated farm-level gross margin can increase by over USD 101/year/ha (including the purchase of inputs and services) compared with current levels (USD 25/year/ha). This is a significant increase from the current level of USD 8/year/ha in 2018 terms), or 63% of the National Poverty Line (USD 162/person/year). This is a direct measure of increased resilience to poverty.


The planned investment Project is expected to yield high returns even when considering key risk factors such as: yield and price changes, adoption rates, and project delays. As part of a risk management plan, it is particularly important that the project be implemented in phases with an emphasis on early implementation and phased roll-out forward. Part of the risk management plan could also be to ensure that planned CUSPs are used for their full capacity and that they can be scaled up as demand increases. This will help to reduce the risk of over-implementation and make the project more viable. Close monitoring and support for target farmers and communities to implement water management plans could also help to mitigate this risk. The phased roll-out delays may be controlled with close monitoring and by ensuring implementation does not lose momentum.


The full economic and financial analysis is provided in Annex E.

Economic and financial justification for the concessionality that GCF provides

Despite Ethiopia’s high level of poverty, the country has one of the highest rates of child distress as high, the Ethiopian Government has agreed to take on significant debt for this project, with USD 100 million in loans to be provided by DA and a further USD 25 million from other sources. The GCF is providing USD 348 million in grants and USD 37 million in grants is provided as co-financing, bringing the percentage of grant funding in the co-finance to 24%. USD 58 million in grant funding will be used for the establishment of CUSPs. This represents a significant increase from previous GCF funding will be used for the introduction at scale of climate smart agriculture. While some of the individual activities are new, they have been introduced on a small scale and are not new to Ethiopia. However, many of them are new to the target population of smaller farmers in Ethiopia. In particular, the implementation of such measures in conjunction with other projects is expected to overcome some of the institutional and policy barriers that were once barriers to a degree sufficient that upon project end it is expected that the measures will continue to be implemented without concessional finance. However, for their introduction at scale highly concessional funding is essential. The

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 69 OF 96

## Initial validation of the package of measures is being conducted as part of SLMP2. GCF required is to scale up implementation in all watersheds covered by RLP subsequent to the pilot phase, in which the package is being validated in 30 watersheds as part of SLMP2.

While some farmers will receive some benefits as a result of the implementation of this package of measures, these beneficiaries are vulnerable smallholder farmers facing food, land tenure and water insecurity. Any benefits derived will be used to increase food and water security and cover other basic needs. Due to the high risk aversion among these farmers, they may not be able to take out loans from financial institutions or pay back the cost of any loans to the beneficiaries.

Activities for the expansion of SLMP2: The development of income opportunities and resilient livelihoods as well as those proposed in the pilot phase will still be funded by GCF finance. 

The Economic Net Present Value (ENPV) is US $3,512 million discounted at 5% over a 25-year period (ETB 90.7 billion discounted at 8% over a 25-year period), with an internal rate of return (IRR) of 6%, and a payback period of 5 years. In economic investment analyses, the project therefore meets the World Bank requirement for a minimum IRR of 10%.

Financial viability for this investment in the long run is ensured because once the barriers to implementation of sustainable land management and climate smart agriculture practices promoted by the project have been removed and the farmers are able to access credit facilities, they will be able to increase their farm productivity and hence income rise. These practices that are implemented at the farm level will have short payback times that will motivate farmers to continue with them. The project also ensures financial viability through the establishment of a fund that will be established to ensure that communities continue to maintain collective infrastructure that has been established. The revenue generated from this fund will be used for maintenance and repair works on the infrastructure, thereby ensuring long-term maintenance. Private sector development will mean households will see a sustainable increase in income as they are able to access credit facilities to finance their investments in land management and climate resilient agricultural practices introduced by the project.

By supporting the establishment of financially viable enterprises in the area, the Project helps build resilience and financial sustainability for smallholder farmers who will be able to cover future capital maintenance costs, but only if the commercial interest rate is below their FIR of 10.2% and FIRR of 15%. The project also ensures that the cost of investment is covered by the GCF grant. If private sector investments are available at this rate, but not the size of loans required, CSBRs can, however, improve their ability to access credit facilities from financial institutions through the provision of a small amount of government grant, or reducing their initial working capital requirements. To be financially viable, the CSBRs will require project support in terms of capacity building and business development services as well as a marketing strategy; this increased level of return would also enable them to cover estimated future capital maintenance costs.

Ongoing sustainable land management activities in Ethiopia have resulted in the development of a number of guidelines and manuals that are being used by various stakeholders. These include those developed under the existing guidelines are under development.


The following guidelines will be used to ensure that best available technologies and practices are applied in the project:

8. ESEP Manual (in Amharic, Tigrinya and Oromiffa languages - Amharic, Oromiffa and Tigrinya)
9. Value Chain Development in SLMP Context
10. Business Plan Template
11. Training Manual for FTC Support (HR)

Quality assurance and sustainable delivery of training is ensured by the SLM Best Practices Task Force. The SLM Best Practices Task Force was established in August 2011 with a view to expediting the process of screening, monitoring and evaluating SLM practices and providing technical support to stakeholders in identifying appropriate SLM approaches that are suitable for their specific conditions and management. By 2015, it is expected that there will be over 300 SLM best practice examples and 9 SLM approved practices with high practice potential. The screening criteria to help categorize and prioritize SLM practices are described in Annex A.

The SLM Best Practices Task Force has the following responsibilities:
- To pre-screen and approve SLM practices for inclusion (training, workshops);
- To pre-screen the list of existing SLM practices against the established criteria;
- To identify appropriate SLM practices for training and workshops (validation workshop);
- To submit screened and approved SLM practices to the SLM TC for approval;
- To provide technical support in screening SLM best practices as per the description form provided.

After the SLM Best Practices Task Force has completed its discussions, the Natural Resource Management Directorate (or the case team or coordination unit of the Natural Resource Management Directorate) must take over the responsibility of both continuing an effective system of best practice documentation and building capacity of staff and other stakeholders.

## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Before describing the arrangements (e.g., annual performance report) laid out in this section, please note that the project does not specify a format for monitoring and evaluation. Please provide details of types of interim and final evaluations. Describe Accredited Entity (AE) project reporting relationships, including to the NAFDAC Point and National Rural Land Administration. The institutional arrangements for M&E should be described in detail. The description should relate to the frequency of reporting on project indicators, implementation challenges and financial status.

The institutional arrangements for M&E will encompass four levels that are well aligned with the RLIP institutional and implementation arrangement:

### Federal Level
The federal level sets the expectations for what is to be accomplished in M&E and oversees that capacity building activities are being implemented. The team at this level includes a Monitoring, Evaluation Specialist who ensures that data collected meet quality standards; reviews aggregated field data for analysis and pulls out program level results; compiles and analyzes data from field visits and other sources of information; prepares reports to the government and donors and provides feedback to stakeholders. The Federal M&E team will include: a Senior Monitoring and Evaluation Officer, a Monitoring and Evaluation Specialist, a Data Management Specialist, and a Documentation/Planning and Reporting Specialist. The team also will provide technical assistance to develop a new monitoring system for the RLIP project. This includes training on data collection (e.g., survey design topics including advanced excel, data analysis and reporting, and evaluation practice); a functional web-based data management system in English and local languages), which will help to aggregate mobile application data and collect geo spatial data.

### Regional Level
The regional level leads the rollout of the M&E system to the field; builds skills and capacity at regional and field level stakeholders; ensures that data collected meet quality standards; aggregates field data to analyze results, compiles and analyzes data from field visits and other sources of information; prepares reports to the government and donors and provides feedback to stakeholders. The regional team will include a M&E Specialist who will be responsible for: training on data collection (e.g., survey design topics including advanced excel, data analysis and reporting, and evaluation practice); a functional web-based data management system in English and local languages), which will help to aggregate mobile application data and collect geo spatial data.

### Zone Level
RLIP at zone level is the functional unit of the government responsible for implementing the Agriculture and Natural Resources Office. The project will provide budget allocation at the zone level to provide technical support regional technical capacity and mentoring, conduct quality data assessments, provide clear guidance on which data is collected and how it should be used, and ensure that data is being collected in a timely manner.

### Woreda Level
This level indicates watershed needs and completes annual workplans and budgets, making sure that activities get rolled out on time. The woreda team includes the NRM process owner and technical expert, who receive training from the RLIP M&E team at federal or regional levels to ensure consistency in data collection across scale. They prepare reports based on results achieved. Woreda officers are supported by regional and Federal M&E staff (particularly in completing reports).


---
**Page 90**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 86 OF 96

## Community Level
There are several levels of community members who are involved in M&E, but the Community Facilitator (CF) is the main project interlocutor. Foremen/forewomen, nursery operators and self-help group leaders collect data on their respective projects and submit it to the CF. The CF compiles this information and passes it to the Community Watershed Team (CWT). The CWT is a member of the CMF and serves as a secretary. The DA collects data from the CWT and submits it to the Kebele Watershed Team (KWT) for review and approval, and finally sends the approved data to the office worker.

The methodology for monitoring key outcomes of the project is as follows:
- Land use change: using land management practices; this indicator counts as treated the total area of a micro watershed once all the prescribed soil and water conservation measures identified in the relevant Multi-Year Development Plan (MYDP) have been fully implemented.
- Net GHG greenhouse emissions: estimated using the EuKt carbon balance estimation tool, which calculates carbon accumulation or loss from land use change and agricultural output data. The economic lifetime of the project is assumed to be 25 years (5 implementation and 20 post-project years), the same time horizon used in the Economic and Financial Assessment.

Households adopting diversified livelihood activities supported by the project: this is measured as the percent of households engaging in approved, non-traditional activities, relative to the total number of households in the project area. The definition of what constitutes the set of potential non-traditional activities will be set during implementation and based on the baseline data collected from the Kebele Administrative level. This indicator is expected to increase due to extreme weather events and climate change by diversifying livelihood activities and increasing the resilience of natural resources.

A beneficiary survey conducted by an independent 3rd party will be conducted in the first year, at mid-term and at completion of the project. Administered to households as well as at the woreda and kebele administrative levels, the benefits of the project are expected to increase over time due to the diversification of livelihood activities.

An M&E operational manual will be developed that defines the function of the program level M&E system and its nested RLLP level M&E systems. The manual will embed the tracking of the main GCF indicators related to avoided emissions, land use change, and household livelihoods.

See Annex D:3 RLP2 Gender Approach and Annex D:4 RLP2 Gender Action Plan for further information on statements in terms of number of women involved in the activities. Baseline data for Gender are not available; however, the RP provides a shorthand way to track progress (e.g., "10% below baseline of N or 10"). The indicator measures incremental changes/valued throughout project implementation to demonstrate progress.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk assessment and mitigation measures (pages 3-5)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (AML/CFT), corruption, and other risk factors that may impede the achievement of project objectives. Also describe the proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.

For probability: High = high potential for occurrence; Medium = moderate probability; Low = has negligible probability

For impact: High = high potential for adverse consequences; Medium = moderate impact; Low = low impact

Prohibited practices include abuse, conflict of interest, corruption, retaliation against whistleblowers or witnesses, as well as fraudulent, coercive, and abusive behavior.

## Key Financial and Operational Risks

In the identification of key risks and their rating, the size of the project was taken into account. While several risks to achievement of project objectives have been identified, the experience gained during implementation of SLMJP, as well as the significant resources allocated in the past 5 years for coordination and capacity building efforts are expected to be instrumental in implementing measures to address the key financial and operational risk identified below.

- **Political and governance risk:** Although the state of emergency ended in June 2018, sporadic civil unrest in project areas continues to be a risk to implementation. Implementation of SLM activities continues in the highland regions; however, there remains a risk that preparation and/or implementation of the proposed operational plan may be delayed or suspended due to political instability. This is considered a significant capacity for implementation and sustainability risk. While considerable capacity for SLM interventions exists in certain areas, it is not sufficient to address the needs of all communities. The lack of new resources contributes to this risk, which is mitigated through a project design including significant training and coordination at the national level.

- **Fiduciary risk:** Issues related to procurement and financial management have been observed in previous projects. However, implementation of the WB-supported SLMJP has developed significant capacity for procurement and financial management that are currently rated satisfactory and moderately satisfactory, respectively.

- **Stakeholder risk:** This includes (i) weak multi-sectoral coordination, and (ii) risk of potential elite capture of project benefits. These risks are mitigated through a participatory approach to project design and implementation in targeted communities. These are addressed through intersectoral coordination mechanisms at the Federal, Regional and sector levels, strong communication measures, and a grievance redress mechanism.

An Implementation Support Plan has been developed that describes how the World Bank will support the implementation of the risk mitigation measures identified in the risk matrix.

RLP will also contribute to alleviating some of the drivers of civil unrest, including natural resource degradation and rural landlessness and joblessness.

### Environmental and Social Policy

The RLP is subject to the Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy of GT, for the potential social and environmental impacts on humans and sensitive areas (wetlands, forests, natural habitats, etc.). i.e. less adverse, site specific, few if any of them are irreversible or significant in terms of their impact on human health or the environment. The RLP is also subject to the ESMF as well as with the applicable environmental and social safeguard policies of the World Bank. Based on the framework of GT’s ESMF and taking into account the project characteristics, the RLP's social assessment was carried out and updated with the following major objectives in focus:

- Assess key socio-economic factors that require consideration:
  - Identify vulnerable and historically underenveloped groups that may be excluded from the project and be adversely affected as a result, and the necessary impact mitigating measures.
  - Assess the potential adverse impacts of the RLP, and determine whether the project is likely to trigger the World Bank social safeguard policies;
- Recommend on the early stage of project preparation the appropriate measures towards meeting World Bank requirements on social safeguards triggered by the project (RFPs 4.10 and GSP 4.02).

In line with the Ethiopian Government’s decentralization policy, organizational structure and implementation arrangement and with due consideration to the implementation of project activities at the grassroots level, RLP is designed to operate at federal, regional, zone, woreda/kebele levels as well as the beneficiary community level. The monitoring and evaluation system for the RLP is also designed in a way that it can be implemented by the relevant agent to be executed at all levels of the organizational structure. The institutional arrangement includes RLP-related conflict/grievance redress mechanism/GRRM, consisting of community watershed teams, indigenous local institutions, woreda/kebele watershed teams, and people from woreda agricultural and natural resources offices.

## G

**Watered selection guided by the Community Based Participatory Watershed Development Guidelines (CBPWDG) with support from Development Agents (DAs) and Womda experts. The DA will administer/design subprojects applying a simple checklist as for fast track eligibility checking of identified sub-projects. This is done at the community level, followed by a selection process at the sub-district level based on the criteria for selection and prioritization phase. Once the checklist is approved at the sub-district level, the project design will then be developed and submitted to the Womda Focal Person (WFP) for approval. The WFP will review the project design depending on the scale, nature and type of sub-project, will further screen the sub-projects. The Womda Focal Person (WFP) will approve or reject the project design based on the criteria set out in the CBPWDG. If approved, the project is then followed up. And a team led by experts from the Womda Environmental regulatory body will review the screening process and provide feedback to the WFP. The environmental regulatory body passes recommendations and gives clearance and/or certificate of subprojects. The final approval for the project design is given by the Womda Focal Person (WFP). Approval of the WFP, approval, the document is referred to the regional Bureau of Agriculture and Natural Resources (BANAR) with all the accompanying environmental and social screening/documents/files.

Gender Considerations

Land degradation has important gender dimensions. For example, UNDP finds that land degradation increases the pressure on women to find alternative livelihoods and resources for their families, which often occur under increasingly difficult environmental, physical, social, and economic conditions. Women are also challenged by the combination of land and environmental degradation induced fuel wood and water shortage, making their work even more challenging.

Analysis also indicated the constraints to women's access to equitable roles in decision-making concerning land resources and their engagement in sustainable environmental and land management such as (a) insecure land use rights, (b) the low value assigned to labor and subsistence farming, (c) lack of access to credit and (d) lack of opportunities for income generation. The United Nations Conference on Desertification (UNCCD) illustrated that, often women's inequitable access to secure property rights forces them onto marginal, fragile, highly degraded lands.

The Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) emphasize gender equality and empowering all women and girls as not only a human right but also an essential element for sustainable development. Gender equality is a core principle of this part of each of the SDGs as well as being reflected in the stand-alone goal (Goal 5), to achieve gender equality. Providing women and girls with equal access to the natural resource base and equal representation in decision-making, making sure that they have secure land tenure rights, and ensuring their participation in all aspects of development will therefore create opportunities for women's equal rights to economic resources, as well as access to ownership and control over land and other forms of the natural capital, in accordance with Goal 5.

Gender Dimensions of Land Degradation in Ethiopia

Understanding gender aspects of natural resources management is an entry point into reversing environmental and land degradation. Women are responsible for managing natural resources daily in their roles as farmers and household providers; typically, they are responsible for growing home-stocked crops, collecting fuel wood and water. Generally, women have less access to productive agricultural inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers) than men. Women's livestock but have fewer resources (natural, physical and social capital) to adapt to climate change and cope with climate variability. This is because women are often responsible for managing the household food security and their livelihoods but have fewer resources (natural, physical and social capital) to adapt to climate change and cope with climate variability. Women's access to productive agricultural inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers) is also limited. Women's health, productivity, and development, but also contributes to the intensification of existing gender gaps. In addition, based on violence The Ethiopia Demographic and Health Survey (2016) shows that 32% of women ages 15-49 have experienced physical or sexual violence; domestic violence is the most common form of violence towards women.

Gender gaps are amplified when adaptation measures fail to consider specific needs and preferences of women. Further, local cultural norms and practices have a major impact on access to natural resources and the level of engagement in decision-making concerning land resources. Women's participation in agricultural extension services for farmers to producers on average 23% less than their male counterparts in Ethiopia. For instance, women in rural Ethiopia are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming (e.g., home gardening) rather than commercial agriculture knowledge. However, notwithstanding their reliance on natural resources, women have less access and control over land and property ownership compared with men. Women's lack of access to productive agricultural inputs but also contributes to the intensification of existing gender gaps. In addition, women often depend on common property resources for fuel wood, fodder and food. Lack of land and property ownership and control limits women's voice and agency, because assets are an important factor in bargaining power


---
**Page 97**
---

## GRLLP Gender Approach

The operational steps encompass resilience building through soil and water conservation works, enhanced tenure security for women and men, and improved livelihoods. These interventions will be implemented with support in rehabilitated watershed landscapes including creating jobs, organized cooperatives (women or girls only), climate smart agriculture, and the promotion of improved cookstoves. The project will also promote the adoption of improved cookstoves, which will free up women’s time, which could potentially enable them to engage in climate resilient livelihood activities. These activities could include promotion of improved cookstoves, cultivating trees, bamboo handiwork, beekeeping, etc.

The RLLP components will take into account the different roles of men and women in advancing resilient livelihoods at multiple scales, and respond to the unique interests, priorities and needs of women and men in order to close gender gaps. Women and men at all levels of the RLLP decision-making should be involved as key actors in the assessment of project impacts on gender equality and women’s empowerment (GEWE) and the promotion of GEWE. Both women and men need to benefit from a gender analysis that refines their joint participation and equitable benefits from the project activities. The project will also promote the adoption of improved cookstoves, which will free up women's time, which could potentially enable them to engage in climate resilient livelihood activities.

The RLLP program is well aligned with the SLMP-II’s gender mainstreaming strategy, which aims at promoting gender equality and women empowerment by ensuring equal access to resources, land tenure security, and ownership and control of assets; and promote women's voice and agency, which constitute the four pillars of its strategy. An impact evaluation of gender innovations under RLLP is currently being carried out. The gender assessment of SLMP-II will be used as a reference for assessing whether the project has increased male and male participation among beneficiaries and whether women’s abilities to realize equitable benefits from the natural and environmental resources were effectively improved by the project's activities/initiatives.

A Gender Approach and Action Plan is included in Annexes D.3 and Annexes D.4, to address the gender aspects of land degradation and natural resource use. This will be further informed through an assessment of the SLMP-II’s gender mainstreaming strategy, which is currently underway.

## Financial Management

The financial management (FM) arrangements for the proposed project RLLP will be based on the existing FM systems and structures established under SLMP-II. The FM arrangements for RLLP and SLMP II are in line with the World Bank (WB) policies and procedures. This includes the accounting capacity maintained by the implementing entity, which is currently being audited to ensure compliance with the WB’s financial management standards. SLMP II was audited in accordance with the International Standards on Auditing. The audit for the financial year ended July 7,


---
**Page 98**
---

2017 expert independent (clean) opinion and highlighted some internal control weaknesses in the management letter. This includes delays in funds from between federal level and regional and woreda levels, weak control of activities and budget execution, and lack of transparency in the disbursement process for the project in the Woredas.

The project addressed these weaknesses progressively in line with an FM Action Plan agreed with the World Bank (AE) For RLP, the Federal PCU at the MoA will be the overall financial responsibility for the implementation of the project. The Federal PCU will also be responsible for the Regional Bureaux of Agriculture (BoA's) and the administrations of all the implementing woredas. Annual project budgets will be prepared by the Federal PCU, approved by the World Bank, and then allocated to the BoAs and the woreda level. Disbursements are based on the approved budget and accounted for using quarterly internal financial reports from each implementing unit. The disbursement process is subject to a rigorous audit system. Audits will be conducted annually by the Supreme Audit Institution or an accredited private audit firm. The audit will be conducted in accordance with the World Bank's Procurement Regulations and other relevant laws and regulations to ascertain whether project funds have been used for the intended purpose. The AE reviews and provides clearance for the disbursement of funds from the BoAs to the woredas. At the woreda level, the BoA will ensure that the six regions and all woredas will maintain segregated local currency bank accounts where project funds will be deposited and disbursed in accordance with the approved work plan and budget. The BoA will also monitor disbursement into each of the local currency project accounts based on the approved annual work plan and budget. In addition, the BoAs will ensure that all payments are made through the appropriate channels for direct reimbursements, direct payment and special commitments.

To enhance the level of disbursements under the new reporting system, the BoA will provide a monthly progress report to the AE. The final financial statements and financial reporting for the proposed project will follow international financial reporting and auditing standards. The FM rating for the implementation of RLP is considered Substantial. An FM assessment of the implementing entities including the BoAs and woredas will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank's Procurement Regulations and other relevant laws and regulations. A Project Implementation Strategy Document (PISD) under the project will be prepared by the Federal PCU, which will include a detailed implementation plan for each of the six regions. D. Procurement under the project will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank's Procurement Regulations and other relevant laws and regulations. The PISD will also include a Project Implementation Strategy Document (PISD) under the project will be prepared by the Federal PCU, which will include a detailed implementation plan for each of the six regions. D. Procurement under the project will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank's Procurement Regulations and other relevant laws and regulations.

The PISD will also include a Project Implementation Strategy Document (PISD) under the project will be prepared by the Federal PCU, which will include a detailed implementation plan for each of the six regions. D. Procurement under the project will be carried out in accordance with the World Bank's Procurement Regulations and other relevant laws and regulations.

Taxation: Where goods and services are procured by the project, these will be subject to income tax, import duties, withholding tax and Value Added Tax. GCF proceeds can be used to pay taxes.

Authorization for project implementation: The World Bank will sign a financing agreement with the Federal Democratic Republic of Ethiopia. Once this agreement is signed, authorization for project implementation will need to

be obtained from Ethiopia’s House of Parliament. During project implementation, the Steering Committee will have oversight of the project. Steering committee members include various Ethiopian government bodies, as described in Section C.7.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP136

Proposal name: Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project

Accredited entity: World Bank

Country: Ethiopia

Project/programme size: Large


## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

### Strengths
- High mitigation and adaptation impacts.
- Substantial potential for cost-effective mitigation interventions at USD 6.8 per tCO2e.
- The adaptation impact is also very high, benefiting over 10 million direct beneficiaries, and over 26 million indirect beneficiaries.

### Points of caution
- The project scales up results of past interventions in sustainable land management to 1:10 climate change risk watersheds, which is a successful baseline of smaller projects by the Government.
- The Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project takes a bottom-up approach at landscape scale, and is aligned with Government strategies, which can bring a paradigm shift from top-down and scattered interventions.

### Ethiopia
Ethiopia is a least developed country with five alternative sources of concessional finance. The Ethiopian Government of Ethiopia to pursue its ambitious programme of landscape restoration and it targets 10 million hectares of vulnerable watersheds and communities.


---
**Page 106**
---

The project will generate considerable environmental, socioeconomic, and sustainable development benefits (including market integration) and responds to several Government of Ethiopia’s social and economic development priorities.

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the short-term addendum XIII titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations.”

## Summary of the Secretary's assessment

### 2.1 Project background

#### Background
1. **Background: Ethiopia** In the highlands of Ethiopia, climate change is expected to increase annual precipitation and seasonal variability in rainfall, increasing soil erosion by 7 to 10 per cent per year and, in the more extreme scenarios, possibly by as much as 40 to 70 per cent.
2. **Impact on Agriculture** The projected increase in temperature will reduce agricultural crop productivity in Ethiopia by 5 to 10 per cent by 2030. Land degradation in Ethiopia has proceeded at an alarming rate and will be intensified with climate change, affecting approximately one-third of the country (47 million hectares) of land has been degraded, affecting a population of 20.65 million, approximately one in three people.
3. **Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project** The Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project (RLLP) will improve climate resilience by increasing the availability of water for irrigation, improving access to diversified sources of income in selected vulnerable rural major watersheds in the regions of Amhara, Benishangul Gumuz, Oromia, Southern Nations Nationalities and Peoples’ Region (SNNPR), Tigray, and Addis Ababa.
4. **Innovative Approaches** The RLLP will scale up initiatives with demonstrated climate value and co-benefits within the past World Bank (WB) projects in Ethiopia. The project will also pick new innovations via WB Proposed interventions targeting rural livelihood productivity and resilience to climate change, including: improved water harvesting and storage practices; enhanced land tenure; gender-sensitive livelihood initiatives that contribute to removing barriers to women’s ownership of and control over assets, and the strengthening of value chains for agricultural products.
5. **Project Scale** The project will target 12 major watersheds with an area of up to 12 million hectares in Ethiopia. The benefits of RLLP include the entire population of the selected watersheds, estimated at 4.2 million people, or 87,049 households, conservatively estimating that 53 per cent are women and 60 per cent are children under five years old.
6. **Project Interventions** The project interventions are also expected to lead to a greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions reduction of approximately 12 million tonnes CO₂ equivalent by 2030, through carbon sequestration as an input of improvements to grasslands, afforestation and agriculture.

#### Implementation
7. **Implementation Framework** The RLLP will be implemented through four integrated components, which, taken together, will address the key drivers of vulnerability in Ethiopia’s major watersheds for vulnerable rural populations in Ethiopia. Component 1 forms the core of the project and includes a range of interventions to improve water harvesting and storage practices.
8. **Component 2** It also includes co-financed activities addressing household energy services. These activities are essential to change the development pathway of rural Ethiopia to one in which


---
**Page 107**
---

## Climate Risk and Adaptation Country Profile: Vulnerability, Risk Reduction and Adaptation to Climate Change: Ethiopia

**GFDL/2017/TA/AM-08**

Page 157

### Introduction

Land use is climate resilient. Component 3 will create institutions and build capacity that will enable the interventions introduced in component 1 to be sustainably implemented even after watersheds graduate from project-based support. Component 3 deals specifically with the barrier of institutional capacity, which is a major constraint for Ethiopia. Capacity building is essential to motivate them to implement the new practices that will be promoted by RLIP. Without this, the new practices introduced in component 1 and 2 are likely to be abandoned by the proposed project will be abandoned once project support ends. Finally, project management activities are covered by component.

### Droughts

Over the past three decades, Ethiopia has experienced countless localized drought events and seven major droughts, five of which have been associated with famine. The most significant agricultural drought occurred in 1973-1974 in the northern region. Most of the recent drought and flood events have been geographically concentrated into two broad zones of the country, with the eastern and northern areas being the most vulnerable. For example, the 2015/2016 drought was particularly severe in the Afar region, while food insecurity and famine in the Tigray region in the north of the country. There are numerous observed changes in rainfall patterns over recent decades, including a decrease in total annual rainfall and a tendency towards lower rainfall during the main growing seasons (March-May and December-February). These changes have been attributed to both natural variability and anthropogenic Indian Ocean warming. While floods have historically never been a major economic risk for Ethiopia, they can cause significant socio-economic disruption due to flooding, for example in 1997 and 2006.

### Financing Information

The project will finance activities in the watershed category. The project is estimated at US$ 34 million. The project lifespan is 25 years. USD 107.17 million in high-conviction financial loans is requested, with an additional USD 9.8 million to be provided by the World Bank (WB) and USD 66 million in non-conventional sources. The project budget includes USD 100 million in loan to be provided by the WB as an International Development Association (IDA) credit. In addition to IDA, Japan will provide a grant of USD 25 million. The German government will provide GIZ-administered Multi-Donor Trust Fund (MDTF), with contributions from the Government of Norway and the European Union. The project also includes parallel financing, parallel financing (which is not included in the project financing) is provided from the Government of Canada through MDTF financing. The Board of the World Bank has already approved its funding and started implementation of preparatory activities in April 2018.

### Accredited entity

The accredited entity (AE) has categorized RLIP as environmental category B, noting that significant changes to the environment are not expected to occur due to the nature of the proposed project activities. The AE submitted a detailed environmental management framework (EMF), and a resettlement policy framework (RPF), and a social

**Page 108**

2.2 Component-by-component analysis:

### Component 1: Investment in green infrastructure and resilient livelihoods (total cost USD 222.5 million; GCF contribution USD 90.8 million; loan USD 86.5 million; GCF covers 40 per cent of total cost)

This component will provide support for the restoration of degraded landscapes in selected watersheds and help build resilient livelihoods through a series of activities organized as three subcomponents:

- **Subcomponent 1.1, land restoration and watershed management:** Implement sustainable land management practices in line with multi-year development plans in watersheds, including land rehabilitation measures and establishment of green infrastructure (e.g., terraces, check dams). This will also include soil conservation measures that ensure reduced surface run-off and soil erosion, as well as improved land productivity. Activities may include the implementation of agroforestry systems, soil and water conservation measures; gully rehabilitation; establishment of green corridors; sustainable land management use; establishment of plantation blocks; and restoration of degraded landscapes.

- **Subcomponent 1.2, climate-resilient agriculture:** Enhance livelihood resilience of beneficiaries through climate-smart agricultural practices in combination with broader climate-smart agriculture activity packages comprising one or more of the following: farm water and soil management; integrated soil fertility and land health management; improved livestock production; sustainable crop-livestock integration; livestock production through feed development and management; and intercropping.

- **Subcomponent 1.3, diversification and value addition:** Further increase livelihood resilience by diversifying livelihoods, and help ensure livelihood sustainability by better connecting products with value chains in selected watersheds through the establishment of producer groups or cooperatives. This will include grants to common interest groups and financing activities that facilitate private sector engagement, either supported either directly or through primary cooperatives and/or cooperative unions.

### Component 2: Investment in institutions and information for resilient livelihoods (total cost USD 29.7 million; GCF contribution USD 14.85 million; loan USD 14.85 million; GCF covers 50 per cent of total cost)

This component will strengthen institutional capacity and improve information for better decision-making in supporting resilient landscapes and diversified rural livelihoods in the project area through the following programme of activities organized as two subcomponents:

- **Subcomponent 2.1, capacity-building, information modernization, and policy development:** Strengthen the institutional capacity of local governments at all levels to implement the project and build capacity to sustain land and water management practices in watersheds, including financing of selected staff positions, financing of technical assistance for the implementation of the project, and the establishment of pilot sites for new technologies for information modernization (such as the use of electronic maps, drones, and other remote sensing tools; and the use of unmanned aerial vehicles for land certification mapping), and development and application of a regulatory framework for the establishment of water user associations and community

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential

**Scale: High**

The BILDP is expected to have significant mitigation impact. The project will result in a net carbon sink of 43.0 million tCO2eq emissions over a period of 25 years, resulting in 1,752 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent being sequestered from the atmosphere. Detailed calculations and scenarios have been prepared using the Ex-Ante Carbon-balance Tool. Most of the carbon sequestration comes from increased use of fertilizers, herbicides, and pesticides for crop production and building construction, resulting in a net sink.

- **Indirect beneficiaries:** The potential of the project is significant. The number of direct beneficiaries includes the entire population in the selected watersheds and is estimated to be 4.2 million people, or 83.0% of the total population as per the 2017 census. Indirect beneficiaries include just over 25 million people in Ethiopia. This scenario is conservative as it is based on 2007 population census figures, whereas the population has been estimated to have increased by about 40%. The project will also contribute significantly to increase climate resilience in 210 major watersheds with up to 12 micro-watersheds per major


---
**Page 110**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium-High

17. RLPF presents an integrated package of activities that is the result of the extensive experience gained from the successful projects of Ethiopia and the WBI in promoting SLM which is essential to achieving paradigm shift. While project activities are not innovative in the global context, they are highly relevant for Ethiopia as they address the specific challenges related to the population of smallerholder farmers already operating in highly degraded landscapes, which include: (i) poor cropland management practices; (ii) rapid depletion of vegetation cover; (iii) poor soil fertility and low crop yields. The project addresses these issues through an approach grown out of the project’s theory of change by delivering more productive, secure livelihoods to local communities and by establishing the institutional framework needed to support SLM at scale. By building on the experience gained from Ethiopia’s successful projects and local governments, RLPF will lead to a durable shift towards sustainable land management (SLM) in Ethiopia. The project is designed so that it addresses the root causes of degradation; only some of the drivers of degradation are addressed might lead to temporary, local improvements but not necessarily to long-term solutions. This is particularly true for post Ethiopian farmers.

15. RLPF presents important opportunities for scaling up and replication. A crucial barrier to achieving a national level of investment required to restore all degraded watersheds nationally is the need to demonstrate a strategy for the long-term maintenance of these restored, newly productive lands. The project will address this by providing technical assistance and market incentives for long-term SLM and by investing in robust impact evaluation, knowledge management systems, and policy dialogue. This will provide the evidence needed to scale-up of SLM for climate change adaptation and mitigation. In the shorter term, replication of the successes of RLPF interventions can also be expected in neighboring watersheds, a process that has already begun with the successful scaling up of improved land use practices as a result of informal dissemination of improved land and water management practices.

3.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: High

20. Environmental benefits of the project include improved water management including increased soil moisture and reduced variability in response to flood/drought conditions. Soil retention will increase crop yields (resulting in higher income, more resilient livelihoods for vulnerable communities in degraded watersheds as a result of investment in irrigation systems, improved land use practices, and land certification). The project activities that are focused on improved croplotes (not financed by GCF) will also increase the food security of communities, and prevent a return to food insecurity due to climate shocks, resulting in social and economic benefits in treated watersheds. Project-funded demonstration plots have already shown significant increases in yields.

22. Gender-sensitive development impacts will result from the promotion of women’s participation in community watershed associations, specific targeting of women in issuance of


---
**Page 111**
---

### Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

23. Ethiopia is a least developed country that is among the most vulnerable to climate change and its adverse impacts. Its capacity to reverse climate impacts on its economy is low; it is sensitive, and its adaptive capacity is low. In the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative country ranking, Ethiopia ranks 148 out of 152 countries. The poverty headcount has fallen from 55.3 per cent in 2007 to 26.7 per cent between 2000 and 2016; these gains are very fragile in a changing climate. Resulting agriculture is a high priority, as agriculture accounts for about 40% of GDP and employs over 85% of the population. Ethiopia ranks among the top 10 export commodities by value: Ethiopia’s Second National Communication identified the primary drivers of vulnerability to climate change as droughts, floods, and pests that severely fed agriculture. The project directly targets the vulnerable populations, particularly smallholder farmers in the Amhara region.

24. The proposed project has been requested by the government of Ethiopia to both scale up the success of the ongoing SLMP and to introduce new, transformative and innovative elements to address climate change impacts on agriculture and food security. The national Climate Resilient Green Economy (CRGE) strategy has called for annual spending of USD 5 billion over a period of five years in order to achieve its goals. The total cost of climate-relevant actions estimated to be in the order of USD 460 million per year and international support is required to bridge this gap. The country faces significant financing gaps. Poor access to credit, high lending rates and an insufficient budget are not conducive to the investments required for handling local climate change impacts. In addition, Ethiopia’s Debt Sustainability Analysis (DSA) indicates that a large proportion of its debt is concessional. More concessional financing, including a high degree of concessionality, is needed to ensure improved resilience in the country.

25. Ethiopia's long-term goal is to ensure that climate change adaptation and mitigation are fully mainstreamed into development activities. The proposed project is designed to be transformative and innovative by addressing the key challenges identified under the Growth and Transformation Plan II, the Climate Resilient Green Economy strategy, and accompanying 2015 Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP). It will also contribute towards Ethiopia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), the 2017 national adaptation plan (NAP) to address climate change, the Ethiopia Climate Resilience Program (ECRP), the Green Growth and Development Strategy (GGDS), REDD+plus strategy, as well as sector strategies for energy, water and agriculture. The proposed project is in line with the intention of Ethiopia to link its net GHG emissions to 2016 to 145 tCO2eq or lower. The Government of Ethiopia requires financial support in order to deliver on these targets.

### Country ownership

#### Scale: High

26. Ethiopia has not yet submitted a country programme to GCF. The country has received a readiness grant to support the definition of key components of a country programme between 2015 and 2017. The project concept notes, which are required for the development of project concept notes to be submitted to GCF, are expected to be concluded in 2020.


---
**Page 112**
---

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Medium: High

13. The economic net present value is USD 3.3 billion Discounted at 5 per cent over a 25-year period. The internal rate of return (IRR) in cash flow terms is therefore 47 per cent with a payback of 5.3 years. The project is therefore highly cost effective.

14. The appropriateness of GCF concessionary rents on the need to address market failure and provide public goods. The provision of public goods is clear in the institutional and information asymmetry. However, the proposed management activities under subcomponent 1.1 are meant to correct market failures related to the management of land resources and the use of agricultural inputs. The use of grant funding could be questioned for the climate-smart agriculture investments in activity 1.2 which is not a public good. In fact, the proposal states that the problem of information asymmetry that leads to an increased pricing of risk in these investments. Indeed, the funding proposal states that existing commercial banks of sufficient size are unavailable to make these investments. If alternative private sector investments are demonstrated, it will support sustainability of the project and its outcomes.


---
**Page 113**
---

13. Ethiopia's debt situation and the long payback period of the project are also considered.
14. The project is highly cost effective, as evidenced by a base case EIRR of 47 per cent. ### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies
#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards
15. Environmental and social risk category: The World Bank categorizes the Resilient Landscapes and Livelihoods Project (RLLP) as a Sustainable Category I, owing to significant adverse environmental and social risks that are not expected to occur due to the nature of the proposed project activities. The potential adverse environmental impacts on humans and sensitive ecosystems are considered low in probability, site specific, and few, if any, would be irreversible. The Secretariat confirms the classification as consistent with GCF policies. The Secretariat recommends a risk-based approach to be employed, and the fact that the interventions themselves are designed to reduce risks of adverse environmental impacts. The project has been demonstrated in Sustainable Land Management Project (SIMP II). The overall environmental impact is considered low because the proposed activities are well defined and easily managed. Cumulative adverse impacts on biodiversity and ecological change-induced imbalances would be of very low probability.
16. Social safeguard category: The Secretariat confirms that the project complies with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards and requirements.

75. The Secretariat notes that the proposed project activities are within the process of screening, assessment review and approval of safeguards in terms of environmental and social safeguards. If the project is approved, the overall likelihood of occurrence and/or impacts of the project are generally either of low magnitude or low probability of occurrence; these could also include: (i) possible failure to consider the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities due to lack of consultation; (ii) possible outbreak of diseases due to ecological change; (iii) possible impact on biodiversity due to inadvertent habitat destruction; (iv) possible negative social impacts due to displacement; (v) possible triggering of conflicts over land boundaries between individual group claimants; (vi) short-term environmental and social risks associated with project implementation; and (vii) construction-related risk on labour management, safety and social concerns.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project interventions are designed to reduce water use and energy consumption in agriculture. This is also expected to lead to greenhouse gas emissions reduction due to carbon sequestration as a result of improved soil health and reduced tillage. Air pollution would be minor for this project and would come mainly from small-scale construction activities which can be managed by good housekeeping and proper waste disposal. These should be minimized through the use of low-emission vehicles, equipment, and machinery in accordance with the ISMP. The risk of environmental contamination with pesticides from the application of synthetic chemical pesticides is reduced due to the promotion and application of the World Bank Policy on Pest Management, which promotes the use of Integrated Pest Management (IPM) techniques. However, if there is a significant increase in the use of pesticides and agrochemicals, the policy will be triggered by the RLLP activities, even though RLLP itself does not promote the use of synthetic chemical pesticides.

However, in the course of activities related to agriculture and water harvesting structures, the introduction of high-value crops and use of pesticides, introduction of new varieties of crops, new farming techniques, and the application of agrochemicals and organic fertilizers (insecticides, herbicides, fertilizers, etc.). The project promotes the use of integrated pest management practices and other alternative pest control practices that seek to reduce reliance on synthetic chemical pesticides. It involves (i) managing pests through cultural methods such as crop rotation and intercropping; (ii) eradicating them; (iii) relying, to the extent possible, on non-chemical measures to keep pest populations at a level below economic injury levels; and (iv) using biological control in a way that minimizes adverse effects on beneficial organisms, humans, and the environment.

### ESS 4: Community health, safety and security

The project poses a risk to community health through exposure of workers to hazardous substances. The proposed project intervention specifically modifies the landscape and ecology of the area. This includes possible changes in water quality and quantity due to construction activities related to pesticide use and handling is also being addressed as part of the sub-project guidelines on IPM through training in pest and fertilizer applications, and safe-chemical handling, among others. The project will be implemented within a 50-metre radius from existing wells less than 45 metres and will use FAO Manual on Small Earth Dams: A guide to sizing and construction, approved by the Ministry of Agriculture Guidelines for Dams and Reservoirs to ensure safety of small dams.

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The AL has submitted a RFP to address land acquisition and involuntary resettlement. It is indicated that resettlement impacts for the project would be rare since ground activities will be


---
**Page 115**
---

implemented in a community-based participatory manner and lands to be used for facilities are mostly communal lands or obtained through voluntary donation. Still, there is a possibility of undue pressure on individuals from community leaders or peers, to donate land for the conservation project. The RFP also provides that the project will not include any lands included in the RFP to ensure land donations are voluntary. There is also a possibility of reversion of communal lands by the people who donated them and their use for other land use, which will be addressed through a consultation process with the affected groups to find alternative arrangements. The project may also cause potential individual conflicts over land boundaries and access rights, especially in areas where there are existing or emerging disputes over boundaries. The SA noted the existence of customary conflict mediation institutions that have been established by communities in SLM areas for use in the settlement of disputes involving rural lands. These institutions have been successfully tapped in SLM as well as other projects to mediate conflicts and ensure smooth implementation. These institutions have also made part of the grievance redress mechanism under SLM.

### 41. ESS: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

Although the project is expected to help reverse the degradation of biodiversity resources, there are several risks that could undermine this objective. These include introduction of exotic tree species in the reforestation activities, or from the intermixing of agricultural and forest land uses. The effects of these changes on the ecosystem may be largely unknown impacts on certain species due to change in moisture availability. There may also be a need to continuously monitor changes in the biological profile of the project area throughout its life cycle as some of the introduced tree species may not survive or occur. Ecological change could trigger an outbreak of endemic diseases or reemergence of latent diseases, which could have serious consequences for the health and productivity of the project successfully alters or "improves" the landscapes and ecology of the area. Alterations to existing land uses can also lead to changes in water availability and quality, thus potentially affecting vector-borne disease epidemiology. The management measures include screening out any subjects that would (a) remove or convert natural vegetation; (b) degrade critical habitats"; and/or (c) "cause loss of biodiversity", and, in terms of siting and design, ensuring that the project does not have significant adverse impacts by re-siting, rerouting or modification of design, and, where necessary, ensure (e) minimization of cutting of trees; (f) preparation of wildlife management plan; and (g) careful selection and use

### 47. Environmental and Social Safeguards Building

The ESMP indicated that the implementation of the project's activities and the environmental and social safeguards will be implemented through a multi-level approach, from the national level down to the local or community level. Overall coordination and implementation of the project will be facilitated by the MoA in collaboration with other relevant ministries while at the regional level, the implementation of the ESMP will be coordinated by the DRSs. The National Project Coordinator (NPC) will be responsible for ensuring that all environmental and social safeguards aspects of subproject preparation, review and approval will also be addressed.

### 48. Stakeholder Engagement

The project will be implemented largely through a community-based participatory approach. Hence, engagement of communities with the project is ensured at every stage of implementation. The project will ensure that there are opportunities for the conduct of consultation, participation and engagement of all stakeholders, including local communities.

### 49. Grievance Redress Mechanism

A grievance redress mechanism is described in the ESMP and RFP. The AE has an established grievance redress mechanism and the project provided a summary of this process to ensure that any issues or concerns raised by IPs and other stakeholders can be resolved. The GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy also states that the GCF Independent Project Accountability Mechanism (IPAM) will be available for all IPs, with a focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### 4.2 Gender Policy


---
**Page 117**

10. The Act has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

11. The gender analysis describes the enabling environment that supports the work on gender equality and women's empowerment. Ethiopia has ratified international and regional instruments related to gender equality, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Women and adopted several institutional and policy measures. Ethiopia’s Constitution, various policies, and laws provide for equal rights between men and women in areas such as property and land use. Regional laws are being amended to improve women's benefits from land resources and increase their access to credit. The government is also working on increasing access to climate sensitive technologies and enhancing women’s access to alternative sources of energy (e.g., improved access to biogas, solar power, and wind). Moreover, the government is striving to reduce the barriers to women’s ownership of land and property rights and improve women's voices and agency as well as increase economic empowerment for women. It also strives to ensure that all development interventions are gender-sensitive and that they deliver gender-sensitive services.

12. The analysis provides detailed information on the gender situation at the national level and in the region where the project is to be implemented. National data on gender indicates that, despite policy commitments, there are gaps and challenges to women’s empowerment and inclusion in decision-making processes and access to resources and communication, although there is gradual progress in some aspects. The gender inequality situation on the ground is influenced by a combination of factors including cultural norms, traditional practices and perceptions that continue to constrain women's access to livelihood options, particularly natural resources. Such constraints negatively affect women’s capacity to adapt to climate change.

13. In all six regions women are mostly responsible for reproductive roles such as taking care of children and the elderly, cooking, cleaning, and fetching water and firewood. Findings of focus group discussions with women revealed their triple work burden because of their domestic responsibilities and lack of access to productive resources. Women do not engage in activities outside the home, thus limiting their capacity to generate income. However, further focus group discussions indicated that young married women and girls saved several hours per week from the time spent collecting firewood for successful adoption of improved cook stoves at other homes, and the adoption of the cookstoves alleviated some of routine housework burdens. Perceptions among women were that they had more time to engage in income generating activities engaged in all farm activities on home gardens. For female heads of households the perceptions were that they had more time for their own business ventures because they allowed farmers to work their land in return for splitting the produce. In addition, men have lower expectations from women and do not allow them to participate in decision-making regarding activities to produce what they grow themselves, such as small ruminants and vegetables. Roles with greater economic value are often assigned to men while women are expected to develop skills and bargaining power. Married women and female heads of households have time constraints, while young women indicated that they have the time and a high degree of interest in engaging in income generating activities. Women who were married for less than 5 years said that wives spend more time than their husbands in project-related work though they have lower financial returns from these activities. The women also reported increased knowledge on tree planting, improved vegetable and potato production, and natural resource management bylaws, among others.

14. In the six regions, women’s groups are active in 20 of the 36 districts. Women’s platforms accounted for only 18.34 per cent in 2016, indicating a large gap in achieving the 30 per cent quota set by law.

15. The Act, as per the requirement of GCF's Gender Policy, has submitted a gender action plan that has taken into account the challenges faced by women and the lessons learned from the previous phase of the project. The gender action plan includes a set of activities that address

# Results monitoring and reporting

The theory of change (TOC) provides clearly articulated the roots causes driving the high vulnerabilities to climate change. The four RILP components are linked to the eventual short-, medium- and long-term outcomes and overall impacts.

However, the "if then because" logic does not clearly show in the diagram as provided in the most recent version of the TOC. For example, the assumption that if the project proposes the outcomes and overall impacts is not fully established. As an example, a key assumption is that if more people have access to land tenure security, they will be able to adopt sustainable natural resource management and improvements in livelihoods. However, the project proponent does not clearly articulate how this will happen or what the enabling factors are.

Furthermore, the critical element of behavioural change is not mentioned at all in the TOC. This assumption should be articulated as an important element in driving the changes, especially for the institutional strengthening component which is linked to natural resource management practices. Additionally, the activity that would enable and support this set of assumptions (e.g., capacity building) is not clearly linked or explained.

Finally, in terms of institutional strengthening, the critical assumption for the particular element of land tenure security is not articulated as an inherent issue in the existing legal framework which would enable the issuance of land certificates and improve the quality of service delivery. This could be a robust impact evaluation component which could mitigate the issues noted above.
